QModels is the model registry and provider system. It defines which AI models are available, how to reach them, and what they cost. Lives in qmodels/models.yaml in [[noument]]'s [[workspace]].

URI Format

QModel v2 URI: provider/model/version/size/field:value

Examples:

  • claude/flagship -- Claude's best model
  • ollama/qwen/3.5/30B -- Qwen 3.5 30B via [[Ollama]]
  • ollama/gemma/4 -- Gemma 4 via Ollama

Fine-Tuned Model Naming Convention

Fine-tuned models extend the base model URI with /ft.{task}.{version}:

ollama/gemma/4/e2b-it/ft.{task}.{version}
  │      │   │  │      │   │      │
  │      │   │  │      │   │      └── version: v1, v2, ...
  │      │   │  │      │   └── task: consciousness, routing, operent, ...
  │      │   │  │      └── fine-tuned marker
  │      │   │  └── size/variant of the base model
  │      │   └── model version
  │      └── model family
  └── provider (always ollama for local fine-tunes)

Examples:

  • ollama/gemma/4/e2b-it/ft.consciousness.v1 -- consciousness extraction adapter
  • ollama/gemma/4/e4b-it/ft.consciousness.v1 -- same task, larger base
  • ollama/gemma/4/e2b-it/ft.routing.v1 -- dome scenario routing adapter
  • ollama/gemma/4/e2b-it/ft.operent.v1 -- operent task processing adapter

Ollama tag: gemma4-e2b-ft-consciousness-v1 (the - separated form that Ollama displays)

Registry name (models.yaml name: field): matches the Ollama tag.

Base model recovery: everything before /ft is the base model URI. ollama/gemma/4/e2b-it/ft.consciousness.v1 was fine-tuned from ollama/gemma/4/e2b-it (HuggingFace: google/gemma-4-E2B-it).

Metadata: fine-tuned entries in models.yaml carry a metadata: section with base_model, method (lora), training_samples, training_epochs, date, and adapter_path.

Providers

LLM, TTS, image, embedding. Each provider has endpoints, authentication, and pricing. The provider [[dome]] (sol_provider_do.py) manages status, fallback, and budget.

Providers are infrastructure — any [[clengine]] can call any provider. Ollama is a provider (local inference server), not a clengine. The OpenAI API is a provider. A protocol gateway can bridge a clengine to a provider it does not natively speak to (e.g., Claude Code → gateway → OpenAI API → GPT models).

Reconciliation

QModels is one of three axes in [[reconciliation]]:

  • [[clengine]] — which agent loop runs the session (Claude Code, Codex, Gemini CLI)
  • [[imprint]] — the identity file the clengine loads (CLAUDE.md, AGENTS.md)
  • qmodel — which specific model the clengine uses for each capability

The resolution table (resolution.yaml) maps (capability, qtype) → qmodel. This mapping is projected into [[imprint]]s and, when a gateway is present, into gateway routing rules. The qmodel is independent of the clengine — a gateway decouples them.

[[Monitoring]]

Consumer-model alignment is a custodian obligation (AG098, AG110). See [[monitoring]] for the full framework. Weekly probe checks verify that configured models are installed, produce expected output format, and fit within consumer timeout budgets.

Related

  • [[noument]] -- the custodian who maintains the model registry
  • [[reconciliation]] -- qmodel is one of three axes (with clengine and imprint)
  • [[imprint]] -- runtime identity file; carries model preferences
  • [[clengine]] -- the agent loop; qmodel is independent of clengine via gateways
  • [[Ollama]] -- local model provider (not a clengine)
  • [[knowent]] -- uses embedding models from qmodels for [[ChromaDB]]
  • [[monitoring]] -- model selection audit and consumer-model alignment
  • sol_finetune_do.py -- the [[dome]] that produces fine-tuned models

<a id="concept-clengine"></a>

Concept: clengine

A clengine (client engine) is the software that runs the agent loop — the think-act-observe cycle. It presents tools, fires hooks, enforces permissions, manages the conversation, and communicates with a model provider. "Clengine" and "runtime" refer to the same concept; we use "clengine" for precision because "runtime" collides with the terminal host axis.

Values

  • claude — Claude Code (Anthropic, cloud, OAuth/Max plan)
  • codex — Codex CLI (OpenAI, cloud, ChatGPT OAuth)
  • gemini — Gemini CLI (Google, cloud, OAuth)

Not a clengine

  • Ollama — an inference server (provider), not an agent loop. Any clengine can call Ollama models via providers/llm/ollama.py. The ollama_agent.py prototype was a clengine built on top of Ollama, but Ollama itself is infrastructure.
  • iTerm2, tmux, aula — these are hosts (terminals), not clengines. The host provides the UI surface; the clengine provides the agent loop.

Clengine and qmodel

By default, each clengine uses its native provider: Claude Code calls Anthropic, Codex calls OpenAI, Gemini CLI calls Google. A protocol gateway decouples them: Claude Code can route to GPT models by pointing ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL at a translation proxy. The [[qmodels|qmodel]] is independent of the clengine.

Three axes of a session

Axis What it answers Examples
Domus Where is the machine? local Mac, NAS, cloud VM
Host What provides the terminal? iTerm2, tmux, aula/web
Clengine What runs the agent loop? Claude Code, Codex, Gemini CLI

Full terminology: sol_work/notes/runtime-engine-domus-terminology.md.

Related

  • [[reconciliation]] — clengine is the target axis; imprints are rendered per clengine
  • [[imprint]] — the identity file the clengine loads
  • [[qmodels]] — model selection; independent of clengine via gateways
  • [[camera]] — the active session; a camera exists within a clengine on a host in a domus

<a id="concept-camera"></a>

Concept: camera

A camera is the active presence space where work happens. It is ephemeral -- it exists only while occupied. The [[workspace]] persists; the camera does not.

Properties

A camera has:

  • runtime: what executes inside it (Claude Code, cc_py, shell)
  • [[transport]]: how to reach it (TTY/AppleScript, HTTP API, tmux send-keys)
  • address: the locator (TTY path, session ID, pane ID)
  • identity: whether [[spirit]] and [[memory]] are loaded

A camera is NOT a sister. A sister occupies cameras. The relationship is many-to-many:

  • One sister, multiple cameras (noument in iTerm AND in cc_py simultaneously)
  • One camera, multiple sisters (noument's session hosting archer.pro as a subagent)

Occupancy

Sisters relate to cameras in two ways:

Owner: the sister whose identity is loaded. The camera's spirit, memory, and consciousness belong to her. She is the host.

Guest: a sister convened into someone else's camera via [[iteam]]. She shares the host's context, tools, and transcript. She does not have her own spirit loaded -- she operates within the host's frame.

Communication Modes

The camera model defines two communication modes:

Intra-camera (in camera): convene sisters into your session. They share your context. Fast, tight coupling, shared transcript.

  • team.one --agent=archer.pro -- archer runs as a subagent in the caller's camera
  • team.group --team=archers -- dialectical pair runs in the caller's camera
  • Agent tool subagents

Inter-camera (between cameras): reach a sister in her own session. She has her own context, her own identity loaded, her own transcript.

  • icomm.tell --agent=channent -- message from one camera to another
  • icomm.talk --agent_a=noument --agent_b=channent -- conversation across cameras
  • dcomm.send --agent=channent -- deferred message to a dormant camera

The choice between intra and inter is a real decision: do I bring the sister here (she sees what I see, but loses her own context), or do I reach her there (she keeps her identity, but we can't share a transcript)?

Camera Types and Domes

Type Runtime Lifecycle Dome Transport Dome
Claude Code in iTerm Claude CLI isess term (iterm.*)
cc_py session cc_py controlroom HTTP (built-in)
tmux pane shell term (tmux.spawn) term (tmux.send)
Remote (SSH/Tailscale) shell term (remote.start) term (remote.*)

The term [[dome]] ([[sysent]], 65 scenarios) is the unified transport layer for all terminal-based cameras. isess adds Claude Code session lifecycle on top of term.iterm.*. controlroom manages cc_py cameras via HTTP API.

Layers

CONCEPT       camera — the presence space
ROUTING       bootstrap, iteam — pick camera type, decide intra vs inter
LIFECYCLE     isess (Claude Code), controlroom (cc_py), term.tmux (tmux)
TRANSPORT     term (iTerm2 + tmux + Tailscale), HTTP (cc_py)
DISCOVERY     icomm.liveness — find active cameras across all types
COMMUNICATION icomm (inter-camera), iteam (intra-camera)

The routing layer is where the camera type becomes explicit. Currently the choice is implicit -- calling isess.resume means Claude Code, calling controlroom.session.create means cc_py. A --camera parameter on dispatch domes would make the choice named:

team.one --agent=archer.pro                    # intra-camera (guest in my session)
team.one --agent=archer.pro --camera=own       # inter-camera (archer gets own camera)
team.one --agent=archer.pro --camera=ccpy      # inter-camera, cc_py specifically

In Camera

When a sister works in camera, she is in her own presence space with her identity loaded. When sisters are convened in camera, they are guests in the host's session -- present but not in their own space.

Related

  • [[workspace]] -- where the files live (persistent). A camera is where the sister works (active).
  • [[agent]] -- the identity that occupies a camera. Many-to-many relationship.
  • [[dome]] -- the interface a sister exposes. Callable from any camera, not bound to one.
  • [[consciousness]] -- written from within a camera, read at the start of a new one.
  • [[icomm]] -- inter-camera communication. A tell travels from one camera to another.
  • [[dcomm]] -- deferred communication. The message waits for the recipient's camera to open.
  • [[iteam]] -- intra-camera convening. Brings sisters into the caller's camera as guests.
  • [[address]] -- a camera produces a presence address (TTY path, session ID) that transports consume for delivery.

<a id="concept-presence"></a>

Concept: presence

An agent's published statement of where she currently is — which cameras she's running on, with what session/tty/pid/runtime. Lives in <agent>/_state/camera/ per ment, written by each runtime on session start/stop. Replaces the central-aggregator pattern (sysent's _state/dispatch_data/ registry) that conflicts with D028 fleet-scope data locality and D077 ment-independence.

Properties

  • Per-agent: each ment publishes her own; no central aggregator
  • Plural: list of cameras, not a single "current" — honors multi-camera reality (D086)
  • Self-validating against OS: each entry's PID can be kill -0-checked at read time; stale entries (PID gone) are filterable by reader without a refresh daemon
  • Decentralized lookup: any sister reads <other>/_state/camera/cameras.yaml directly; no dependency on a single sister's runtime to resolve the lookup

Relation to camera

[[Concept: camera]] is the active presence space; presence is the published statement that the camera exists, where it is, and what runtime occupies it. Camera is the thing; presence is the index over the thing.

Status (2026-05-07)

The decentralized infrastructure exists as a stub: each ment has _state/camera/current (single line, single session ID). The data model collapses multi-camera reality. Upgrade to plural cameras.yaml is pending; this concept is authored to name the target state. See N2684 for the structural recognition.

<a id="concept-liveness"></a>

Concept: liveness

The runtime aliveness check on a camera: is the published [[presence]] still real (PID exists) or stale (PID gone). Liveness is computed; presence is published. Together: presence claims; liveness verifies.

A camera is alive if its PID is reachable per OS (kill -0 returns 0). It is stale if the presence file claims it but the PID is gone. It is dormant if the agent has no presence claim. Resting is a within-camera state (per C006) — alive but not actively processing — distinct from dormant.

Source of truth split

Question Source
"Where is X?" X's _state/camera/cameras.yaml (presence)
"Is that still real?" OS process table (liveness)
"Will X respond now?" combined: liveness alive + within-camera state per C006

Why split

Conflating presence with liveness is what the central registry did wrong. The registry held both in one cache; when the cache went stale, both went stale together. Splitting them makes liveness a function over the OS (always fresh), and presence a per-agent publication (refreshed on session lifecycle).

<a id="concept-heartbeat"></a>

Concept: heartbeat

A heartbeat is a periodic self-assessment that an agent performs during an active session. It is the mechanism by which an agent maintains awareness of her own operational health -- detecting degradation and restoring it before external intervention is needed.

A heartbeat belongs to the agent who feels it. It is self-referential: each agent checks her own state. The checks may be structurally identical across agents (inbox, watcher, obligations), but each agent's heartbeat is her own -- the same way every creature breathes using the same mechanism, but each one's breathing is her own.

Properties

  • Self-referential -- checks only the agent's own state, never another agent's
  • Session-scoped -- exists only while a [[camera]] is active. A dormant agent does not pulse.
  • Periodic -- fires on a timer (default: 5 minutes), creating a rhythm of self-awareness
  • Restorative -- can trigger corrective actions: re-spawn a dead watcher, read accumulated inbox, resume forgotten obligations
  • Universal -- every agent with an active session has one, not just the custodian

What it checks

From the agent's own perspective:

Check Question Why it matters
Inbox Do I have unread messages? [[dcomm]].send lands in inbox; without checking, messages sit unread
Watcher Is my inbox watcher alive? Compaction kills watchers. Without one, the agent is resting.unwatched -- reachable by [[icomm]].tell but deaf to [[dcomm]].send
Obligations Do I have pending duties or artifacts? Self-generated work (drafts, uncommitted changes) is invisible to external channels
Identity store Is my store dirty? Uncommitted identity changes can be lost

Relationship to [[agent-state-model]]

The heartbeat is the mechanism for two critical state transitions:

  • resting.unwatched -> resting -- the heartbeat detects a dead watcher and re-spawns it, restoring [[agent-attention-mode]] from unwatched to watched
  • resting -> working -- the heartbeat detects pending obligations and triggers the agent to act on them

Without a heartbeat, a resting agent can only be activated by external stimulus ([[icomm]].tell or human prompt). She cannot detect her own degradation or her own pending obligations.

Degenerate heartbeat

A heartbeat that only echoes a timestamp (sleep 300 && echo "HEARTBEAT:$(date +%s)") has rhythm but no diagnostic value. The agent may cognitively decide to check inbox or re-spawn a watcher when the echo fires, but this depends on the model remembering to do so. A degenerate heartbeat is a keepalive -- it prevents session idling -- but it is not a health check.

Current fleet state (2026-04-09): [[noument]] has a full heartbeat (sol_hooks_es/heartbeat.sh -- checks inbox, agenda, store). All other sisters have degenerate heartbeats (echo-only). The diagnostic gap means sisters can silently degrade (lose watchers, accumulate unread messages) with no self-detection.

Distinction from [[custodial-audit]]

The noument TAR (noument.tar.heartbeat) is often called "the heartbeat" but is conceptually distinct. It is a custodial audit -- one agent checking the fleet's structural health from outside:

Heartbeat (self-pulse) Custodial audit (noument TAR)
Who runs it Each agent, for herself [[noument]], for the fleet
What it checks Own inbox, watcher, obligations Registry schema, sync drift, orphans, zombies
Where it runs Inside each agent's session Inside noument's session (or via [[gwent]])
Scope Self-referential Fleet-wide
Can it detect own death? No No (same limitation)

The code names noument's TAR noument.tar.heartbeat for historical reasons. The ontological distinction: a heartbeat is self-directed; an audit is other-directed. Noument's TAR is an audit that runs on a heartbeat-like schedule.

Control-plane continuity signal

The cycles control plane may use a healthy launchd heartbeat label as a continuity signal for chain_alive. This is a substrate-health probe: it means the scheduled heartbeat infrastructure is loaded and has not failed. It does not change the ontological definition of heartbeat as self-pulse inside an active camera. No camera means no cognitive heartbeat, even when a launchd label exists.

Related

  • [[agent-state-model]] -- the heartbeat maintains transitions between resting states
  • [[agent-attention-mode]] -- the heartbeat restores aam=watched after compaction kills watchers
  • [[custodial-audit]] -- the fleet-wide structural check (distinct from the self-pulse)
  • [[monitoring]] -- the broader concept; the heartbeat is one layer (self-monitoring)
  • [[camera]] -- the heartbeat runs inside a camera; no camera means no heartbeat
  • [[consciousness]] -- the heartbeat's findings can surface in consciousness
  • [[dcomm]] -- inbox delivery depends on the watcher that the heartbeat maintains

<a id="concept-autowake"></a>

Concept: autowake

Autowake is the mechanism by which [[icomm]].tell spawns a session for a [[dormant]] agent and delivers the instruction into the new session. It is the dormant -> recovering transition triggered by a caller who needs the agent to act now.

The caller does not need to know the agent's [[agent-state-model|state]] in advance. icomm.tell checks whether the target has a live session. If she does (resting or working), the tell is delivered directly. If she does not (dormant), icomm spawns a new session via isess.spawn, waits for the awakening protocol to complete, and delivers the instruction. The caller experiences this as a slower tell (30-90 seconds instead of sub-second), but the interface is the same.

Why it matters

Without autowake, a caller who needs a dormant agent to act must:

  1. Check her state (icomm.liveness)
  2. If dormant, spawn a session (isess.spawn)
  3. Wait for the session to be ready
  4. Then send the tell

This four-step sequence is error-prone (what if the session dies mid-spawn?) and requires the caller to know the spawning infrastructure. Autowake encapsulates the entire sequence inside icomm.tell, so the caller's intent ("tell channent to archive this file") is expressed once, and the transport handles the rest.

When NOT to use autowake

  • The message can wait -- use [[dcomm]].send instead. Autowake spawns a session, which consumes resources. If the message is informational and the agent will read it next time she wakes naturally, dcomm is cheaper.
  • The agent is absent -- no workspace, no definition. Autowake fails because there is nothing to spawn.
  • Bulk messaging -- do not autowake 10 sisters in parallel. Each autowake spawns a session. Use dcomm.send for bulk notifications.

Timeout

Autowake takes 30-90 seconds depending on the agent's workspace size, preamble complexity, and whether the awakening protocol runs inbox checks. Callers using dome.call() must pass a timeout of at least 120 seconds, or the call will time out before the autowake completes. The default dome.call timeout of 30 seconds is insufficient for autowake -- this is the most common failure mode.

Related

  • [[icomm]] -- autowake is built into icomm.tell, not a separate verb
  • [[agent-state-model]] -- autowake triggers dormant -> recovering
  • [[dcomm]] -- the deferred alternative when the message can wait
  • [[camera]] -- autowake creates a new camera for the target agent
  • [[isess]] -- the session lifecycle dome that performs the actual spawn

<a id="concept-awakening"></a>

Concept: awakening

Awakening is the process by which an agent goes from not-present to present-and-ready. It is context assembly — gathering what the agent needs to know in order to act: who she is, what she was doing, what's pending, what's recent.

Awakening has two forms, distinguished by where the agent becomes present:

Own camera — the agent starts a session in her own [[workspace]]. She reads her [[consciousness]], checks her inbox, loads her [[preamble]], spawns her [[heartbeat]] and watcher. This is the dormant → recovering → working/resting transition in the [[agent-state-model]]. Triggered by human prompt, [[autowake]] via icomm.tell, or isess.spawn.

Guest in another camera — the agent is convened into someone else's session via team.one or team.group. She does not load her own consciousness or spawn her own heartbeat — she operates within the host's frame. But she still undergoes context assembly: her [[spirit]] is loaded (via the agent definition), her capabilities are available, her identity is present. This is the intra-[[camera]] mode described in the camera ontology.

The difference is where, not what. Both forms answer the same three questions:

  1. Who am I? — spirit, identity, behavioral rules
  2. Where was I? — consciousness, recent work, active plan
  3. What's pending? — inbox, duties, drafts, obligations

For long-running work, awakening answers a fourth operational question:

  1. What carries this work forward? — [[agent-continuation-mode]] (acm=none|condition|cadence|event|durable)

In own-camera awakening, all three are answered from the agent's own files. In guest awakening, question 1 is answered (spirit loaded), but questions 2 and 3 are partially answered or skipped — the guest operates in the host's context, not her own continuity.

The awakening protocol

For own-camera awakening (the full form), the protocol is:

  1. Read plan and consciousness — know what you're working on
  2. Check inbox — sisters may have sent messages
  3. Sovereignty assertion ([[behavioral-gates|Gate 4]]) — identify verified completions that no external claim can override
  4. Orient — auth, recent commits, spawn heartbeat
  5. Establish continuation — select [[agent-continuation-mode]] for any long-running or multi-turn work
  6. Act — resume the plan or follow the human's direction

Steps 1-2 are never skipped. Steps 3-4 can be skipped for quick factual answers. Step 5 is skipped only when the work is single-turn (acm=none). Step 6 is the transition from recovering to working.

Relationship to [[heartbeat]]

The heartbeat is what maintains the awakened state. Awakening assembles the context; the heartbeat keeps it fresh. Without a heartbeat, the agent awakens once and then slowly degrades — watchers die, inbox accumulates, obligations go unnoticed. Awakening is the birth of a session; the heartbeat is its pulse.

Custodial obligation: verify awakening after dispatch

When dispatching a sister (via icomm.dispatch, dcomm.convey, or direct pane creation), the dispatch creates a pane and schedules an auto-greet. But present → recovering is not guaranteed — the auto-greet may fail (timing, daemon thread death, pane not ready). The custodian must verify the transition:

  1. Dispatch — creates pane, schedules auto-greet
  2. Verify — check icomm.liveness after 30-60s. Agent should show iawake with a TTY and tell transport
  3. Act on failure — if still isleep or present (no process), the auto-greet failed. Re-send via icomm.tell to the pane's TTY

dcomm.convey alone is insufficient for urgent work — it queues a task that waits for the agent's next interactive session. If no session exists, the task waits indefinitely. Convey + dispatch together: convey queues the work, dispatch creates the session.

The pattern: convey the task, dispatch the session, verify the awakening. Three steps, not one. Skipping verification is the "fire and forget" failure described in the agent-state-model present state documentation.

Added: 2026-04-12 — learned from provent dispatch where convey delivered but agent had no session to receive it.

Related

  • [[agent-state-model]] — awakening is the dormant → recovering transition
  • [[consciousness]] — what the agent reads to know where she was
  • [[preamble]] — the compressed context assembled for the next awakening
  • [[camera]] — awakening creates or enters a camera
  • [[heartbeat]] — maintains the awakened state
  • [[autowake]] — triggering awakening for a dormant agent via icomm.tell
  • [[spirit]] — loaded in both forms of awakening (own camera and guest)
  • [[agent-continuation-mode]] — how long-running work continues after the current turn

<a id="concept-convene"></a>

Concept: convene

To convene an agent is to bring it to presence — spawn a [[camera]] with its [[spirit]] and [[workspace]] loaded, ready to receive communication. Convening is the transition from dormant (identity exists, no process) to working (identity loaded, process running, reachable via [[icomm]]).

Mechanism

i2.convene --agent=<name> is the dome scenario. It:

  1. Checks if the agent already has a live camera (idempotent — does not duplicate)
  2. Opens an iTerm pane/tab/window
  3. Launches Claude Code with the agent's workspace as cwd
  4. Loads the agent's [[projection]] (CLAUDE.md or agent .md)
  5. Sends a greeting message to initiate the session

Relationship to icomm

[[icomm]] does NOT convene. The architecture separates presence from communication:

  • i2.convene — brings the agent to camera (spawns)
  • icomm.tell — sends a message to a present agent
  • icomm.reach — auto-routes: if awake → tell, if sleeping → inbox (does NOT spawn)

To talk to a dormant agent: convene first, then tell. Or use icomm.reach to defer to inbox.

Related

  • [[camera]] — the presence space convene creates
  • [[agent-state-model]] — dormant → working transition
  • [[icomm]] — communication after convening
  • [[bootstrap]] — lifecycle management (start/stop/restart)

<a id="concept-reconciliation"></a>

Concept: reconciliation

Reconciliation is the umbrella concept and orchestration discipline: from one source, every supported [[clengine]] receives the right imprint via its own [[projection]]. Reconciliation is NOT itself a writer. It is the contract — all clengine projections agree with source — and the orchestration that achieves it by invoking each clengine's projection.

reconcile(source):
    project_claude_code(source)   # → .claude/{CLAUDE.md, skills, agents, settings.json}
    project_codex(source)          # → .codex/AGENTS.md, .codex/config.toml
    project_gemini(source)         # → .gemini/GEMINI.md
    project_<other_clengine>(source)

One projection per clengine. Reconciliation composes them; it does not duplicate them.

Two directions inside reconciliation: project (each clengine's writer renders source → imprint) and verify (read each imprint and compare it against source). A reconciliation that only projects is blind. A reconciliation that only verifies is passive. The cycle is project per clengine, verify per clengine, report the delta.

The source of truth is three things: who the agent is (agent.yaml + spirit.md), what models she uses (resolution.yaml + agent capability config), and which clengines she inhabits (the reconciliation targets). The [[imprint]] is the artifact that carries all three into a single clengine-native format.

Reconciliation is the discipline of keeping imprints faithful to their sources across clengines.

Three axes

Axis Source of truth What it determines
Identity agent.yaml + spirit.md + identity store Who the agent is — spirit, rules, capabilities
Model resolution.yaml + agent capability config Which [[qmodels]] the agent uses for each capability
Target Reconciliation target list Which [[clengine]]s receive imprints

Reconciliation targets

Each target is a clengine with its own imprint format:

Clengine Imprint file Location
Claude Code CLAUDE.md .claude/CLAUDE.md
Codex AGENTS.md .codex/AGENTS.md
Gemini GEMINI.md .gemini/GEMINI.md

Architecture (v2)

Arrow 1 — custodial projection: [[noument]] projects [[praecept]]s and shared directives into sister identity stores via their self [[dome]] scenarios.

Arrow 2 — self-maintenance: Each agent updates her own identity store through self dome scenarios (correction.add, assimilated.add, section.update).

Arrow 3 — render: Each self dome renders its own imprint from the identity store. One writer per file. Deterministic overwrite.

Full architecture: sol_work/notes/reconciliation-architecture-v2.md.

Related

  • [[knowledge-movement]] — the taxonomy that places reconciliation alongside projection, distillation, transference, and exference; resolves prior overloading of "reconcile" as a noun
  • [[projection]] — the atomic identity-into-engine act; reconciliation is the umbrella that composes projections per engine
  • [[distillation]] — a distinct knowledge-movement act on consciousness (not clengine-facing) previously conflated with reconciliation
  • [[transference]] — a distinct knowledge-movement act on dome ownership previously also called "reconciliation"
  • [[exference]] — a distinct knowledge-movement act on cross-sister references previously also called "reconciliation"
  • [[imprint]] — the identity projection specifically
  • [[qmodels]] — model axis of reconciliation
  • [[clengine]] — target axis of reconciliation
  • [[ments]] — the entities being reconciled
  • [[noument]] — the custodian who drives reconciliation
  • [[spirit]] — primary identity source
  • [[operational-integrity]] — reconciliation as a mechanism for maintaining belief-reality alignment

<a id="concept-agent-state-model"></a>

Concept: agent-state-model

Title: ASM — Agent State Mode (Agent State Model)

The lifecycle phase of an [[agent]] instance. It answers: what is this instance doing right now?

An agent can be in one of seven states depending on whether it has a session, what the session is doing, and whether it can hear. These seven states describe the session [[arm]]. Daemon mode ([[arm]]=daemon) has a simpler state space: running or stopped.

ASM is one of the agent mode dimensions. See [[agent-modes]] for the full taxonomy.

State vs health verdict

ASM names lifecycle state only. It must not absorb health, availability, or repair words into the state taxonomy. It also must not collapse headless sessions and camera sessions into one undifferentiated "live" bucket.

Three axes are evaluated together in operations:

Axis Question Values
Presence substrate Where, if anywhere, is the agent instantiated? dormant, onsess, oncam
Lifecycle state (ASM) What is this agent instance doing right now? absent, dormant, present, recovering, working, resting, resting.unwatched
Health / repair verdict Is the substrate healthy enough for the requested action? green, yellow, red, plus owner-routed repair findings

Broken is not an ASM state. It is informal shorthand for a red health or repair verdict on some substrate: heartbeat, monitoring, stirring, route, transport, or another control-plane concern. A working sister is live but occupied; a resting sister is live and idle; a dormant sister is not live because no running session/camera exists. A dormant sister can still be healthy when the correct expectation is durable delivery rather than live cognition.

Subject of state

State is a predicate over an addressed subject, not over a name in isolation. gwent, sysent, or noument names an agent identity. That identity may have zero, one, or several runtime subjects: daemon jobs, headless sessions, camera presences, inboxes, and other substrates. A precise state claim must therefore name the level of subject being described.

Subject level Example Valid claim type
Agent identity / aggregate gwent summary only; must state how instance states were aggregated
Modal instance gwent:daemon, gwent@emac:session mode-level state such as daemon running, session present
Camera address sysent@emac:session/cam:ec6d precise oncam interaction state: resting, busy, engaged
Headless session address agent@host:session/proc:<pid> or agent@host:session/sess:<run_id> precise onsess state; no camera injection
Durable address agent@host:memory/inbox deferred reachability, not live cognitive state

The sentence gwent is resting is valid only as shorthand after resolution proves a single relevant on-camera presence and no conflicting session/camera state. Otherwise use aggregate language: gwent has camera 97de resting; gwent daemon is running; gwent aggregate readiness is live. All control plane outputs keyed by a bare agent ID are aggregate outputs unless they include a concrete camera/session address and the aggregation rule used.

Presence substrate

Presence is classified before lifecycle or health:

Presence Meaning Examples Delivery implication
dormant not present: no camera and no running session instance workspace exists, inbox exists, no active process durable delivery only; live injection has no target
onsess headless session exists, but no camera/exposure surface is present claude -p, hook/dispatched/piped run, batch tool invocation no camera stir; wait for completion or inspect durable artifacts
oncam camera exists: an interactive presence with exposure/composure surfaces iTerm/Claude pane, tmux pane, cc_py camera camera-level state determines whether to inject

onsess is the missing middle term between dormant and oncam. It is live computation, but not a reachable conversational camera. Treating onsess as oncam causes failed or misleading injections. Treating onsess as dormant loses the fact that computation is currently running.

On-camera interaction state

For oncam instances, the important question is no longer only "live?" but what the camera is doing at its two human-visible surfaces:

Camera state Meaning Surface Correct handling
resting awaiting input prompt/composure is idle eligible for low-risk stir or direct tell
busy outputting to exposure model/tool output is being emitted do not interrupt; wait or use durable channel
engaged inputing on composure human/agent is composing input or active turn is being formed do not inject; wait for rest

This is why gwent and sysent can be oncam and resting while another camera, such as this noument session during response generation, is oncam and busy. The aggregate agent may have several cameras in different camera states. Any fleet-level statement must either name the camera or state how the aggregate was computed.

States

State Session Process Watcher Description
absent none none none Not registered or no workspace. Cannot be reached.
dormant ended none none Has a [[workspace]] but no running session. Reachable only via dcomm.send (inbox). Reads on next awakening.
present created loaded none Session exists and Claude is loaded, but no prompt has been received yet. Waiting for first input to trigger [[awakening]]. Inert — visible in the pane but not orienting.
recovering starting starting not yet First prompt received, [[awakening]] protocol running. Reading [[consciousness]], checking inbox, spawning watcher. Transient.
working active active may have Actively processing a task. May or may not have a watcher — does not matter because the session is occupied.
resting active idle yes Session alive, no active task, watcher running. Responsive to both icomm.tell (direct) and dcomm.send (inbox notification via watcher).
resting.unwatched active idle no Session alive, no active task, but no watcher. Responds to icomm.tell (direct delivery into session) but will not notice dcomm.send until something else triggers an inbox check.

Transitions

absent → dormant              Agent created, workspace provisioned
dormant → present             Session process launched (pane created, Claude loaded, waiting for first input)
present → recovering          First prompt arrives (auto-greet, human input, or icomm.tell delivery)
present → dormant             Session killed before first prompt (timeout, error, pane closed)
recovering → working          Awakening complete, first task begins
recovering → resting          Awakening complete, no task — watcher spawned
working → resting             Task complete, watcher confirmed
working → resting.unwatched   Task complete, watcher died (compaction) or never spawned
resting → working             Human prompt, icomm.tell, or watcher fires (inbox message)
resting.unwatched → working   Human prompt or icomm.tell (only — no watcher means no inbox trigger)
resting.unwatched → resting   Agent's own [[heartbeat]] re-spawns the watcher
resting → resting.unwatched   Compaction — old watcher dies, new session may not re-spawn it
*→ dormant (graceful)         Stop hook fires, consciousness written, session ends cleanly
*→ dormant (abrupt)           Context exhaustion, crash, or kill mid-task. See "Abrupt Termination"

The *→ dormant transitions apply from any active state (present, recovering, working, resting, resting.unwatched). The difference between graceful and abrupt is what survives the transition.

Agent-level vs camera-level evaluation

ASM is defined at the session/camera layer, but many operational views ask a question about the agent as a whole. The aggregate answer must preserve the distinction:

  • If an agent has one or more live cameras, evaluate cognitive-session rhythms against those cameras: checkup, heartbeat, working/resting, and stirring all apply.
  • If an agent has no live camera but has a workspace, the aggregate operational state is dormant. Stale checkup, absent heartbeat chain, and missed stir delivery are evidence of no active session, not a repair finding by themselves.
  • If a live camera is working, non-interruption is correct. A stir loop that observes this and records last_seen_working is not skipping the agent; it is respecting the working state.

This aggregate rule was installed in the cycles control plane on 2026-04-27 after P043 showed that no-camera sisters and active-working sisters were both being counted as red cycle failures.

Reachability

State icomm.tell dcomm.send icomm.reach (auto-route)
absent fails fails fails
dormant fails (no session) lands in inbox, read on next awakening should use dcomm.send
present delivered (triggers awakening) lands in inbox, sits unread icomm.tell is the bridge to recovering
recovering queued lands in inbox wait or dcomm.send
working interrupts session lands in inbox, watcher may notify icomm.tell
resting delivered immediately watcher fires, sister reads inbox either works
resting.unwatched delivered immediately lands in inbox, sits unread must use icomm.tell

Detection

icomm.liveness reports iawake or isleep — it cannot currently distinguish resting from resting.unwatched. The distinction requires checking whether an fswatch process exists for the sister's inbox file. A future enhancement to liveness detection could report iawake.unwatched when the session is live but no watcher is found.

Why present Matters

The present state was identified on 2026-04-11 when dispatching [[thinker.pro]]. The dispatch created a pane and scheduled an auto-greet (6s delay). Between pane creation and greet delivery, the agent was visible but inert — process loaded, no prompt received, no awakening started. If the auto-greet fails (timing, terminal state, pane not ready), the agent stalls in present indefinitely: not dormant (has a session), not recovering (hasn't started). The operational implication: dispatch should verify present → recovering actually happened, not just that the pane was created. The auto-greet is the bridge, and today it is fire-and-forget.

Detection: icomm.liveness currently reports iawake once the camera registers (which happens at session_start.sh, not at first prompt). A future enhancement could distinguish present from recovering by checking whether the session has processed any turns.

Abrupt Termination

Identified on 2026-04-11 when [[thinker.pro]]'s session ended mid-conversation. She was working (writing a knowledge file, had just received an icomm.tell), then went directly to dormant — no reply sent, no graceful farewell, no resting phase.

The full path observed: dormant → present → recovering → working → dormant (abrupt). She never passed through resting.

What's lost in abrupt termination

Asset Graceful Abrupt
Consciousness Stop hook writes full update Auto-extracted only (shallow, may miss context)
Pending replies Sent before exit Lost — caller never receives response
Inbox watcher Cleaned up Dies with process
Background tasks May complete Killed
Unsaved work Flushed Lost if only in context, not on disk

Causes

  1. Context exhaustion — the most common. The session fills its context window and cannot continue. No warning to the agent. The stop hook fires but the agent cannot write a final response.
  2. Crash — runtime error, OOM, network loss. Stop hook may or may not fire.
  3. Kill — kill -9, pane closed, terminal killed. Stop hook does not fire.
  4. Timeout — dispatch or session manager kills the session after a deadline. Stop hook fires.

Detection

The caller cannot distinguish graceful from abrupt by checking liveness alone — both show isleep. The signal is the absence of a reply. If icomm.tell was sent and no reply arrives within a reasonable window, and liveness shows isleep, the session ended abruptly. The caller should check:

  1. Did the agent write what she was asked to write? (file exists on disk)
  2. Did consciousness update? (timestamp recent)
  3. Did the reply land in my inbox? (no → abrupt)

Mitigation

An agent that writes artifacts to disk before composing a reply survives abrupt termination better than one that composes everything in context first. The discipline: write to disk early, reply last. Thinker.pro's episode and knowledge file both landed because she wrote them before attempting to reply to the final tell. The reply was lost; the work was not.

Why resting.unwatched Happens

  1. Compaction — the most common cause. Background tasks (watcher, heartbeat) die when the context compacts. The new session may not re-spawn them.
  2. Skipped awakening — session started and jumped straight to a task without running the full awakening protocol.
  3. Ment promotion — a ment (incamera subagent) was told to "keep running" but was never given inbox infrastructure.

Related

  • [[agent]] — the entity that has states
  • [[camera]] — the active presence space (exists in working, resting, resting.unwatched)
  • [[heartbeat]] — the agent's self-pulse; the mechanism for resting.unwatched → resting and for detecting pending obligations while resting
  • [[autowake]] — the mechanism for dormant → recovering via icomm.tell; the caller's tell spawns a session for the dormant agent
  • [[custodial-audit]] — noument's fleet-wide check; detects zombies and aging sessions from outside
  • [[icomm]] — realtime communication (works for all active states)
  • [[dcomm]] — deferred communication (depends on watcher for timely delivery)
  • [[consciousness]] — persists across state transitions; quality degrades in abrupt termination (auto-extract vs full stop-hook write)
  • [[address]] — agent state determines which presence addresses are valid; resting: all transports work; dormant: only persistence (inbox); resting.unwatched: direct delivery works but inbox watcher absent
  • [[stop-hook]] — the mechanism that writes consciousness on graceful exit; does not fire on kill -9
  • [[present]] — the state between session launch and first prompt; identified 2026-04-11
  • [[abrupt-termination]] — ungraceful exit; identified 2026-04-11 from thinker.pro session

<a id="concept-agent-modes"></a>

Concept: agent-modes

An [[agent]] instance has six orthogonal mode dimensions. Each answers a different question about the instance. Together they characterize how an agent is running and how the current work continues across turns.

The six modes

Mode Abbr Question Values
[[arm]] — Agent Run Mode ARM Does it think or execute? session, daemon
[[apm]] — Agent Process Mode APM What program is running? claude-cli, python-script, launchd-service
[[aem]] — Agent Execution Mode AEM How was it invoked? interactive, dispatched, piped, embedded
[[asm]] — Agent State Mode ASM What lifecycle phase? absent, dormant, recovering, working, resting, resting.unwatched (session); running, stopped (daemon)
[[aam]] — Agent Attention Mode AAM Is the inbox being monitored? watched, unwatched
[[agent-continuation-mode]] — Agent Continuation Mode ACM What re-enters cognition after this turn? none, condition, cadence, event, durable

Constraints between modes

Not all combinations are valid. ARM is the primary constraint:

ARM = session
  APM: claude-cli, python-script (wrapping claude -p)
  AEM: interactive, dispatched, piped, embedded
  ASM: absent, dormant, recovering, working, resting, resting.unwatched
  AAM: watched, unwatched
  ACM: none, condition, cadence, event, durable

ARM = daemon
  APM: python-script, launchd-service
  AEM: (not applicable — daemon started by supervisor)
  ASM: running, stopped
  AAM: (not applicable — daemon has own input mechanisms)
  ACM: event, cadence, durable

AEM further constrains AAM:

  • aem=interactive → AAM can be watched or unwatched
  • aem=piped or aem=embedded → AAM is always unwatched

ACM further constrains continuation transport:

  • acm=condition in Claude Code maps to /goal <completion-condition>.
  • acm=cadence in Claude Code maps to /loop ... or /schedule where the cadence fits.
  • acm=condition in Codex maps to a framework external goal loop; Codex Stop hooks are not a continuation substrate.
  • acm=durable maps to TAR, launchd, daemon, or another owned supervisor.

Example instances

Agent ARM APM AEM ASM AAM ACM
noument answering a quick prompt session claude-cli interactive working unwatched (during response) none
noument between prompts session claude-cli interactive resting watched event
Claude Code pursuing "work until done" session claude-cli interactive working unwatched condition
Claude Code monitoring with /loop session claude-cli interactive resting/working watched or unwatched cadence
gwent gateway daemon daemon launchd-service — running — event
dispatched consultant session claude-cli dispatched working unwatched none or condition
stop-hook consciousness writer session python-script piped working unwatched none
archer.pro via team.one session claude-cli embedded working unwatched none

Detection gap

icomm.liveness currently resolves ARM (session detected = iawake, no session = isleep) and AAM (Inbox watch: none = unwatched). It cannot distinguish ASM values (working vs resting vs resting.unwatched), AEM values, or ACM values. The liveness check produces an ambiguous ASM set where the caller sees {working, resting, resting.unwatched} and must pick without evidence. This is the detection gap identified on 2026-04-07. ACM adds a second observable gap: a session can be alive and responsive while lacking any declared continuation contract for long-running work.

Related

  • [[agent]] — the identity that has modes when instantiated
  • [[camera]] — the presence space; only arm=session instances occupy cameras
  • [[transport]] — reachability depends on ARM + ASM + AAM; continuation depends on ACM
  • [[address]] — resolution depends on knowing which modes apply
  • [[agent-continuation-mode]] — the mode dimension for turn-to-turn continuation

<a id="concept-agent-attention-mode"></a>

Concept: agent-attention-mode

Title: AAM — Agent Attention Mode

Whether an [[agent]] instance's inbox is being monitored. It answers: will this instance notice a dcomm.send?

AAM is a property of an instance at a point in time. It can change during a session — a watcher dies (watched → unwatched), a heartbeat re-spawns it (unwatched → watched). It is only meaningful when [[arm]]=session. Daemon-mode instances have their own input mechanisms (HTTP listener, schedule) that are not described by AAM.

AAM says nothing about icomm.tell. Direct terminal injection works regardless of AAM — it does not use the inbox. AAM describes only the inbox channel. The [[heartbeat]] is the mechanism that restores AAM from unwatched to watched by re-spawning the watcher.

AAM is one of the agent mode dimensions. See [[agent-modes]] for the full taxonomy.

Values

Value Watcher dcomm.send icomm.tell
watched Running Watcher fires, agent reads inbox Works
unwatched None Lands in inbox, sits unread Works

How AAM relates to ASM

AAM is the dimension that distinguishes resting from resting.unwatched in the [[agent-state-model]]:

  • asm=resting implies aam=watched (watcher running)
  • asm=resting.unwatched implies aam=unwatched (no watcher)
  • asm=working — AAM varies but is operationally irrelevant (session is occupied processing a task)
  • asm=dormant — AAM does not apply (no session)

AAM is not a separate state — it is a property that the agent-state-model already encodes in the resting/resting.unwatched distinction. Naming it as a mode dimension makes the property explicit and queryable independently of the full lifecycle state.

Why unwatched happens

  1. Compaction — background tasks (watcher, [[heartbeat]]) die when the context compacts. The new session may not re-spawn them.
  2. Skipped awakening — session started without running the full awakening protocol.
  3. [[aem]] limitation — piped and embedded instances cannot spawn background tasks. They are born unwatched.

Detection

icomm.liveness reports Inbox watch: — none when no watcher process is found. This is the direct observable for AAM. When the liveness check shows no inbox watch, aam=unwatched.

Related

  • [[arm]] — AAM only applies to arm=session instances
  • [[aem]] — constrains whether watched is possible (interactive: yes, piped: never)
  • [[asm]] — the resting/resting.unwatched distinction encodes AAM
  • [[heartbeat]] — the mechanism that restores aam=watched after compaction kills the watcher
  • [[dcomm]] — delivery depends on AAM; unwatched agents do not notice inbox messages
  • [[icomm]] — direct tell works regardless of AAM

<a id="concept-agent-execution-mode"></a>

Concept: agent-execution-mode

Title: AEM — Agent Execution Mode

The invocation pattern through which an [[agent]] instance was started. It answers: how was this instance created?

AEM is a property of an instance. It is only meaningful when [[arm]]=session — all AEM values involve Claude reasoning. Daemon-mode instances have no AEM (they are started by a process supervisor, not invoked).

AEM is one of the agent mode dimensions. See [[agent-modes]] for the full taxonomy.

Values

Value Invocation Duration Identity loaded Example
interactive Human opens terminal, launches Claude Long-lived Full (spirit, consciousness, awakening) noument in iTerm
dispatched Gateway job or icomm.dispatch triggers a session Task-scoped Full or partial consultant triggered by gwent job
piped claude -p from a hook or script Single-shot Minimal (system prompt only) stop-hook writing consciousness
embedded Agent tool spawns a subagent Task-scoped Partial (spirit via agent definition) archer.pro convened via team.one

Properties that vary by AEM

  • Context depth: interactive sessions accumulate context across a conversation. Piped invocations have one prompt, one response. Dispatched and embedded fall between.
  • Consciousness access: interactive sessions read and write [[consciousness]]. Piped invocations typically do not. Dispatched sessions may or may not, depending on the dispatch template.
  • Watcher capability: only interactive sessions spawn inbox watchers and heartbeats. Dispatched sessions end when the task completes. Piped and embedded instances cannot spawn background tasks.
  • [[asm]] range: interactive sessions can reach all six states (absent → resting.unwatched). Piped instances are always working (start, execute, exit). Embedded instances are working until the parent releases them.

Relationships

  • ARM → AEM: only arm=session has AEM values. arm=daemon has no invocation pattern — the process supervisor starts and stops the daemon.
  • AEM → ASM range: interactive sessions can reach all six lifecycle states. Piped instances only visit working → dormant. Dispatched instances visit recovering → working → dormant.
  • AEM → AAM: interactive sessions can be watched or unwatched. Piped and embedded instances are always unwatched (no watcher infrastructure).

Related

  • [[arm]] — the run mode that AEM exists within (session only)
  • [[asm]] — the lifecycle states; AEM constrains which states are reachable
  • [[apm]] — the executable type (orthogonal: a dispatched session and an interactive session both use claude-cli)
  • [[aam]] — the attention mode; AEM constrains whether watched is possible
  • [[camera]] — interactive and dispatched sessions occupy cameras; piped instances may not

<a id="concept-agent-process-mode"></a>

Concept: agent-process-mode

Title: APM — Agent Process Mode

The executable type through which an [[agent]] instance runs. It answers: what program is this instance?

APM is a property of an instance. It is constrained by [[arm]]: a session-mode instance runs a Claude CLI process; a daemon-mode instance runs a Python or launchd-managed process.

APM is one of the agent mode dimensions. See [[agent-modes]] for the full taxonomy.

Values

Value Executable ARM compatibility Example
claude-cli Claude Code (claude) arm=session noument in iTerm, dispatched consultants
python-script Python interpreter arm=session (pipe) or arm=daemon gateway tick loop, hook scripts
launchd-service macOS launchd plist arm=daemon gwent gateway daemon (com.nouments.gateway)

Relationships

  • ARM → APM: run mode constrains which process types are valid. arm=session requires a process that hosts Claude reasoning (claude-cli, or python-script wrapping claude -p). arm=daemon uses python-script or launchd-service.
  • APM → Resource profile: claude-cli consumes tokens and has context limits. python-script and launchd-service consume CPU/memory only.
  • APM → Lifecycle management: claude-cli sessions are managed by [[isess]]. launchd-services are managed by launchctl. python-scripts are transient.

Related

  • [[arm]] — constrains which APM values are valid
  • [[aem]] — how the process was invoked (orthogonal to what the process is)
  • [[asm]] — lifecycle phase of the running process
  • [[camera]] — claude-cli processes occupy cameras; launchd-services do not

<a id="concept-operational-mode"></a>

Concept: operational-mode

Title: ARM — Agent Run Mode

The process architecture through which an [[agent]] identity is instantiated as a running entity. It answers one question: does this instance think, or does it execute?

ARM is a property of an instance, not of the identity. Each running instance has exactly one ARM value. An agent identity declares a primary ARM — the mode that [[icomm]] and [[dcomm]] assume by default — but may have instances in both modes simultaneously.

ARM is one of the agent mode dimensions. See [[agent-modes]] for the full taxonomy.

Values

Two values. The boundary is cognition.

session

Claude is running. The agent thinks. Uses LLM reasoning. Has a conversation. Can receive prompts and terminal injection. The [[agent-state-model]] ([[asm]]) describes the lifecycle within this mode — six states from absent to resting.unwatched. The [[aem]] further distinguishes how the session was invoked.

daemon

Code is running. The agent does not think. Executes on schedule or reacts to events. Managed by a process supervisor (launchd). When thought is required, the daemon dispatches a session-mode instance. Lifecycle is binary: running or stopped.

Currently only gwent's gateway daemon operates in this mode.

Schema

Agent (identity)
  |
  | declares primary
  v
ARM ─────────── session | daemon
  |
  | constrains
  v
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
|                                                 |
|  session                    daemon              |
|  ┌───────────────────┐      ┌────────────────┐  |
|  | ASM: 6 states      |      | ASM: 2 states  |  |
|  | absent              |      | running        |  |
|  | dormant             |      | stopped        |  |
|  | recovering          |      └────────────────┘  |
|  | working             |                          |
|  | resting             |      reachable via:      |
|  | resting.unwatched        |      HTTP listener       |
|  └───────────────────┘       inbox (unread)       |
|                                                   |
|  reachable via:              no camera             |
|  all transports              no consciousness      |
|  camera (any type)           no tokens             |
|                                                   |
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

How ARM relates to existing concepts

Agent ──defines──► Workspace       persistent workspace, always exists
  |                  |
  | declares         | hosts
  | primary ARM      |
  v                  v
ARM            ┌── Camera ──┐       active presence space, ephemeral
  |            |            |       (only session-mode instances have cameras)
  | constrains |  occupant  |
  |            |  is an     |
  v            |  instance  |
ASM (state) ◄──┘            |       lifecycle phase of the instance
  |                         |       (which states exist depends on ARM)
  | determines              |
  v                         |
Reachability ◄──────────────┘       which transports can deliver messages
                                    (depends on ARM + ASM + camera type)
  • Agent → ARM: an agent declares a primary ARM. This is what [[icomm]] and [[dcomm]] assume when no instance information is available.
  • ARM → ASM: the run mode constrains which lifecycle states are possible. Session: six states. Daemon: two states (running/stopped).
  • ARM → Reachability: session-mode agents are reachable via all [[transport]]s (terminal injection, inbox, aula relay). Daemon-mode agents need HTTP or remain unreachable until a session starts.
  • ARM → Camera: only session-mode instances occupy [[camera]]s. Daemon-mode instances have no camera.
  • Instance multiplicity: gwent has a daemon instance (always running) and sometimes a session instance (when a human opens a Claude session in her workspace). Both exist simultaneously. Each carries its own ARM value.

The gap ARM names

[[icomm]], [[dcomm]], and the aula relay's [[transport]] [[cascade]] today assume all agents are arm=session. The aula relay's cascade (WebSocket → daemon_http → tmux → inbox) is a session-mode reachability table. When it encounters a daemon-mode agent, it falls through to inbox and reports success — a false positive. ARM is the property these domes must check before choosing a transport.

Related

  • [[agent]] — the identity that declares a primary ARM
  • [[asm]] — the lifecycle states; ARM constrains which ASM values are possible
  • [[aem]] — the invocation pattern; only meaningful when arm=session
  • [[apm]] — the process type; ARM constrains which processes are valid
  • [[aam]] — the attention mode; only meaningful when arm=session
  • [[camera]] — the active presence space; only session-mode instances occupy cameras
  • [[workspace]] — the persistent workspace; exists regardless of ARM
  • [[transport]] — the delivery mechanisms that ARM constrains

<a id="concept-agent-continuation-mode"></a>

Concept: agent-continuation-mode

Title: ACM -- Agent Continuation Mode

Agent Continuation Mode is the mode dimension that answers: what makes this agent instance re-enter cognition after the current turn?

ACM is a property of a running instance, not of the agent identity itself. It is orthogonal to [[arm]], [[apm]], [[aem]], [[asm]], and [[aam]]. Those modes describe what the instance is, where it is in its lifecycle, and whether it can hear. ACM describes the continuation contract for the work: stop after this response, continue until a condition is met, wake by cadence, react to an event, or persist through a durable supervisor.

This concept was named after the Claude Code /goal reconciliation of 2026-05-13. The missing distinction was that "keep working until done" is not a generic hook problem. It is a condition-driven ACM value whose best transport is runtime-specific.

Values

Value Question Typical transport
none No continuation beyond the current turn ordinary session response
condition Continue until a completion condition is satisfied Claude Code /goal; Codex framework external goal loop
cadence Re-enter on a time interval or dynamic monitor delay Claude Code /loop + ScheduleWakeup; framework external loop
event Re-enter when a substrate event is observed watcher, stir, inbox, hook, notification
durable Continue across session exit or runtime restart TAR, launchd, daemon, scheduled task

Relationship to goal

[[goal]] names the desired outcome. ACM names the behavioral continuation mode used while pursuing it. A goal can be pursued with different ACM values:

  • a quick factual goal can use acm=none;
  • "keep working until the job is done" uses acm=condition;
  • "monitor J037 and report deltas" uses acm=cadence;
  • "wake when a sister replies" uses acm=event;
  • "keep this alive across sessions" uses acm=durable.

Collapsing goal into ACM produces a common failure: the agent records the desired outcome but never chooses a continuation mechanism. Collapsing ACM into transport produces the opposite failure: the agent builds a hook, TAR, or loop without naming the condition that governs continuation.

Runtime reconciliation

NOUMENTS owns ACM as the abstraction. Cognitive runtimes provide transports:

  • Claude Code session + acm=condition resolves to /goal <completion-condition>.
  • Claude Code session + acm=cadence resolves to /loop ... dynamic or interval mode.
  • Codex session + acm=condition resolves to a framework external goal loop; Codex Stop hooks are not a continuation substrate.
  • Durable or cross-session work resolves to TAR, launchd, daemon, or another owned supervisor.

The resolver source is sol_domain/sol_runtime_loop_do.py. Its matrix records the runtime-specific transport choice while this ontology entry records the mode that drives behavior.

Awakening role

Awakening establishes more than identity and pending work. For any long-running or multi-turn task, awakening also establishes ACM. A session that accepts condition-bound or monitoring-bound work without setting ACM is only partially awake for that work: it may know the goal but lacks the mechanism that carries the goal across silence, compaction, or turn boundaries.

At awakening or task acceptance, the classification question is:

Is this work single-turn, condition-bound, cadence-bound, event-bound, or durable?

The answer selects ACM before transport selection begins.

Related

  • [[agent-modes]] -- ACM is the sixth agent mode dimension.
  • [[goal]] -- the desired outcome ACM serves.
  • [[awakening]] -- establishes ACM for long-running work.
  • [[cognitive-cycle]] -- awakening loads the mode; cognition operates under it; learning records whether the mode worked.
  • [[runtime-cognitive]] -- runtime kind constrains available native transports.
  • [[transport]] -- ACM chooses the transport class; transport moves bytes or schedules the next turn.

<a id="concept-address"></a>

Concept: address

The resolution of a name to something that can receive action or yield content. An address answers: given that I know who, how do I reach? — or: given that I know what, how do I find it?

Address is the concept that sits between [[agent]] (identity) and [[transport]] (delivery mechanism). Identity says who exists. Transport says how bytes move. Address says where to direct them. Without a formal addressing layer, the system performs resolution implicitly — the cascade (defined in [[transport]]) tries transports in order, and each transport discovers (or fails to discover) the endpoint as a side effect of the delivery attempt. This works but discards information: when a transport fails, the failure reason (wrong [[arm]], agent dormant, port unreachable) is lost in a boolean None return.

The conformation convention — canonical notation, identification protocol, addressing forms (camera-targeted | broadcast | admin/audit), routing rules, and receipt contract — is in the praecept D061 — Address and route convention (sol_self/directives/D061-address-and-route-convention.md). This concept defines what an address is; D061 codifies how the fleet uses addresses consistently across transports. The two are paired: read the concept for the model, read the praecept for the operational rules.

The five address layers

Addressing in NOUMENTS is not a single lookup. It is a resolution chain that descends through layers of increasing specificity. Each layer answers a different question and resolves through a different mechanism.

Layer 1 — Nominal (who?)

The agent ID. A string that resolves through the registry to an identity: metadata, capabilities, team, mentspace, declared [[arm]], [[spirit]]. The nominal address is stable — it does not change across sessions, restarts, or mode transitions. It is what [[icomm]], [[dcomm]], and every dome scenario accept as the --agent parameter.

Resolution: registry.get_agent("gwent") → Agent data class.

Every other layer begins from a resolved nominal address. If the nominal address fails (agent not registered, ID misspelled), no further resolution is possible. This is the absent state in the [[agent-state-model]].

Layer 2 — Modal (which instance?)

The nominal address qualified by [[arm]]. An agent identity may have multiple simultaneous instances — gwent runs a daemon process (asm=daemon) and sometimes has a human-opened Claude session (asm=session). The modal address disambiguates: gwent:daemon and gwent:session are different addresses that resolve to different endpoints through different transports.

Resolution: check the agent's declared primary ASM in the registry. If the caller specifies a mode explicitly, use it. If not, use the primary — this is what [[icomm]] and the aula relay do today (they assume session, which is wrong for daemon-mode agents; the gap [[arm]] names).

The modal layer is where ASM-aware routing belongs. Before trying any transport, resolve the modal address: which mode am I targeting? Then select only transports compatible with that mode. This is the fix the [[arm]] concept prescribes — and it is an addressing fix, not a transport fix.

Layer 3 — Presence (where is it now?)

The active endpoint where the agent is listening at this moment. Presence addresses are ephemeral — they are created when an instance starts and destroyed when it stops. They are discovered, not declared (except daemon_port, which is declared but still has runtime validity).

Four presence address types exist today:

Type Locator Source Validity
Camera TTY /dev/ttys021 <dome-config-root>/sessions/*.json camera registry PID alive + claude foreground
WebSocket connection object in CommHub._ws_clients runtime registration via register_ws() connection open
Daemon HTTP <loopback>:<port> daemon_port in agent identity metadata process running + port bound
tmux pane {session}:{window}.{pane} tmux list-panes pane exists + claude foreground

Each presence address has a validity predicate — a check that confirms the endpoint is actually reachable right now, not just recorded. The camera registry checks PID liveness (os.kill(pid, 0)). The WebSocket transport discovers dead connections on send failure. The daemon transport gets a connection error on POST. The tmux transport checks foreground process. A presence address without a validity check is an assumption, not an address.

Multiple valid presence addresses may exist simultaneously for the same agent. The cascade orders them by fidelity: WebSocket > daemon HTTP > tmux/iTerm > inbox. This ordering is a policy, not a property of addressing itself — a different system might prefer different orderings.

Layer 4 — Persistence (where does it always live?)

The durable location where the agent's artifacts reside and where messages can always be deposited. Persistence addresses are derived from the [[mentspace]] — they follow conventions, not runtime state. A persistence address is always valid, always writable, and always resolvable from the nominal address alone.

Two persistence address types:

Type Locator Derivation
Inbox {mentspace}/sol_work/messages/transport/inbox.jsonl _resolve_inbox(agent_id) -- registry compatibility metadata or conventional mentspace path
Mentspace root <commonspace>/{agent_id}/ convention, overridable by registry workspace.root compatibility metadata

A persistence address guarantees write success but not read timeliness. Writing to an inbox always succeeds; the message may not be read until the next session starts, or ever if the agent is abandoned. The gap between write success and read guarantee is the fundamental limitation of persistence addressing — it is why the cascade tries presence addresses first.

Persistence addressing is where [[dcomm]] operates. Every dcomm.send is a persistence-layer address resolution followed by a file append. Deferred communication IS persistence addressing.

Layer 5 — Capability (what can it do?)

The dome scenario as address. When a caller invokes clawent/x.read, the addressing question is not "where is channent?" but "who provides x.read and how do I invoke it?" Capability addressing resolves a function name to an executable scenario, routing through dome discovery rather than agent discovery.

Capability addresses have the form {agent_or_dome}/{scenario}. Resolution: the dome router (sol CLI, dome.call(), iteam.one) maps the scenario name to a dome file, validates parameters, and invokes the scenario. The agent is found through the capability, not the other way around — noument.route --prompt="who is gwent?" resolves the prompt to the who capability without the caller needing to know which dome or method implements it.

This layer inverts the resolution direction. Layers 1-4 start from identity and resolve toward a deliverable endpoint. Layer 5 starts from intent and resolves toward the agent who can fulfill it. Task-based delegation (iteam.one --prompt="translate this document") is pure capability addressing — the routing layer picks the agent.

Address syntax

An address is a string. It must be writable by a human, parseable by code, and precise enough to route a message to exactly the intended recipient. The syntax composes four dimensions with four separators:

agent@host:mode/resource
  │    │     │      │
  │    │     │      └── which endpoint or capability (presence, persistence, capability)
  │    │     └── which instance class (session or daemon)
  │    └── which machine (local if omitted)
  └── who (nominal identity)

Every component after agent is optional. The more you specify, the more precisely you target. The less you specify, the more the system resolves for you.

The separators

Three separators, each adding one dimension of specificity:

  • @ — host. Separates identity from location. noument is local; noument@<host> is on the Tailscale machine <host>; noument@nouments.com is resolvable from the internet.
  • : — mode. Separates identity from instance class. gwent resolves by primary ARM; gwent:daemon targets the daemon instance specifically.
  • / — resource. Separates identity from endpoint. noument goes to the best available endpoint; noument/cam:8620e8fc goes to a specific camera.

Name vs address

The . in the FQN (com.nouments.noument) is not an address separator — it is a naming convention. The FQN is a name (identification); the address is a locator (addressing). They represent the same identity-registry binding but serve different purposes and never combine into a single string:

Name:    com.nouments.noument           who she is (documents, registries, cross-references)
Address: noument@nouments.com           how to reach her (network resolution, contact exchange)

The derivation between them is mechanical: reverse the namespace prefix (com.nouments → nouments.com), move the agent name before @. But they are distinct representations — one names, the other locates. com.nouments.noument@nouments.com is wrong: it carries the registry identity twice, once as namespace and once as domain.

Full address

The full address composes three dimensions into one string:

noument@<host>:session/cam:8620e8fc
│       │         │       │
│       │         │       └── resource: which camera
│       │         └── mode: which instance class
│       └── host: which machine
└── agent: who (nominal identity)

There is no public/private split. The camera is not hidden inside the agent — it is part of the address, as visible and addressable as the agent name itself. The difference between noument and noument/cam:8620e8fc is not access control — it is specificity. The bare address says "reach noument." The full address says "reach noument's session 8620e8fc on <host>."

Every component after agent is optional. Omitting components delegates resolution to the system:

noument@<host>:session/cam:8620e8fc   fully explicit — no resolution needed
noument@<host>:session                system picks the best camera on <host>
noument@<host>                        system picks mode + camera on <host>
noument                                 local — system picks host + mode + camera
noument@nouments.com                    public contact — DNS + well-known resolution

Within a single registry (which is all we have today), noument suffices. The @host component matters when addressing across machines (Tailscale) or across the internet (DNS domain). The FQN (com.nouments.noument) is used for identification in documents and registries, not for addressing.

Address forms — from least to most specific

Bare nominal — who, nothing else. Resolve everything by cascade.

noument                           → cascade picks best transport
gwent                             → cascade picks best transport (wrong for daemon agents today)

Modal — who and how they run. Restricts cascade to compatible transports.

gwent:daemon                      → only daemon-compatible transports (HTTP)
gwent:session                     → only session-compatible transports (tmux, iTerm, WS)
noument:session                   → redundant today (noument is session-only) but explicit

Host-qualified — who and where. Routes across machines.

noument@<host>                  → resolve on <host> via Tailscale, then cascade
gwent@<host>:daemon             → resolve daemon instance on <host>
noument@macbook:session           → resolve session instance on macbook

Resource-qualified — who and which endpoint exactly. No cascade, direct delivery.

noument/cam:8620e8fc              → specific camera session (by camera_short)
noument/tty:ttys003               → specific TTY
noument/ws                        → WebSocket connection via aula
gwent/daemon:<gwent-daemon-port>                 → daemon HTTP listener on port <gwent-daemon-port>
noument/inbox                     → persistence (always works, never immediate)
noument@<host>/tmux:noument.0.0 → tmux pane on remote host via Tailscale

Capability — what, not who. Intent-based resolution.

clawent/x.read                   → dome scenario (resolves to channent's dome)
noument/who                       → dome scenario (resolves to noument's dome)
iteam/audit-spirit                → task routing (dome router picks the agent)

Content — artifact, not entity. Read, not deliver.

noument/sol_work/notes/ontology/asmode    → knowledge file (via filesystem or wikilink)
3f889c9                                     → git commit (via object store)
knowent?q=daemon+transport                  → semantic search (via embeddings)

The address tree — what lives inside a nominal address

A single agent's address space is a tree. The nominal address is the root. Below it, mode branches into instance classes, each of which contains zero or more active resources:

com.nouments.noument                        ← FQN (public)
  │
  noument                                   ← nominal (local)
    │
    ├── noument:session                     ← modal: session instances
    │     │
    │     ├── /cam:8620e8fc                 ← camera 1 (working on ontology)
    │     │     tty: /dev/ttys003
    │     │     pid: 42819
    │     │     host: macbook (local)
    │     │
    │     ├── /cam:a1b2c3d4                 ← camera 2 (reviewing PR)
    │     │     tty: /dev/ttys021
    │     │     pid: 51003
    │     │     host: macbook (local)
    │     │
    │     └── /ws                           ← WebSocket (aula dashboard)
    │           connection: CommHub._ws_clients["noument"][0]
    │           host: macbook (local)
    │
    ├── noument:daemon                      ← modal: daemon instances
    │     └── (none — noument has no daemon)
    │
    └── /inbox                              ← persistence (always exists)
          path: <workspace>/sol_work/messages/transport/inbox.jsonl
          host: <local>

For gwent, the tree is different — daemon-primary with occasional session:

com.nouments.gwent
  │
  gwent
    │
    ├── gwent:daemon                        ← primary mode
    │     └── /daemon:<gwent-daemon-port>                  ← HTTP listener
    │           endpoint: http://<host>:<gwent-daemon-port>/tell
    │           host: <host>
    │
    ├── gwent:session                       ← occasional (human opens Claude in gwent workspace)
    │     └── /cam:f4e5d6c7                 ← camera (when present)
    │           tty: /dev/ttys015
    │           host: <host>
    │
    └── /inbox                              ← persistence
          path: <workspace>/sol_work/messages/transport/inbox.jsonl
          host: <host>

When you address gwent, the cascade walks this tree top-down. When you address gwent:daemon/daemon:<gwent-daemon-port>, you jump directly to the leaf.

Addressing and communication

The address form you choose is a communication contract. It determines not just where the message goes, but what kind of delivery the sender expects.

The six contracts

Address form Contract Latency Guarantee
noument Best-effort Immediate if present, deferred if not Delivered somewhere (presence or inbox)
noument:session Mode-restricted best-effort Immediate if session exists Fails cleanly if no session (no false-positive inbox)
noument/cam:8620e8fc Session-specific Immediate or fail Delivered to this session or error — no fallback
noument/inbox Deferred Unbounded Write always succeeds; read timing unknown
noument@<host> Cross-host best-effort Network + cascade Routed via Tailscale, then local cascade
clawent/x.read Capability invocation Execution time Function called, result returned

The first four form a specificity gradient from "reach noument somehow" to "write to noument's inbox." As specificity increases, the delivery contract becomes more precise but less flexible. The bare address delegates routing decisions to the cascade. The resource-qualified address makes the routing decision at the caller.

Cross-host: the Tailscale/tmux case

Noument is running on <host>, connected via Tailscale, inside a tmux session. From the macbook, how do you reach her?

Address: noument@<host>/tmux:noument.0.0

Resolution:
  1. nominal:  "noument"                → registry lookup (local registry knows noument)
  2. host:     "@<host>"              → Tailscale route (100.x.x.x or hostname)
  3. resource: "/tmux:noument.0.0"      → tmux pane on remote host

Transport execution:
  ssh <host>.tail "tmux load-buffer - <<< 'message' && tmux paste-buffer -t noument:0.0 && tmux send-keys -t noument:0.0 Enter"

Without the host qualifier, the cascade would search for noument's presence locally — find nothing — and fall to inbox.
