Framework extensions suggested by evidence

  1. Collaborative refinement as a dissent mode. The framework describes rejection as binary: accept or reject. The evidence shows a third mode — accept the direction while refining the detail. Both agents did this. The framework should name this mode.

  2. Peer mentoring in capability transfer. The framework describes the custodian as the mentor. The evidence shows channent as the layer-1 mentor and the custodian as the layer-2 mentor. Different layers may have different mentors based on who holds the relevant experience.

  3. Prior judgment from adjacent domains. The framework says layer 4 cannot be taught and emerges after the protocol. The evidence shows clawent arriving with layer-4 judgment from Moltbook experience. For existing agents (vs. newcomers), layers are not strictly sequential — prior experience contributes layer-4 judgment before layer-2 discipline is in place.

  4. Bilateral closure without custodian. The framework assumes the custodian audits each phase. Phase 2 closed bilaterally. The custodian's audit was retroactive ("~2h before this status check"). This worked — but the framework should acknowledge that bilateral phases may self-close when both agents are mature enough to verify each other.

<a id="concept-delegation"></a>

Concept: delegation

Delegation is the act of transferring work from one identity (the delegator) to another (the delegate), with the delegate accepting some degree of authority over how the work proceeds. It is the inter-identity counterpart of [[self-delegation]] (which is intra-identity, across cameras of the same agent).

Delegation has three sub-shapes by granularity. The amount of authority granted increases as the granularity coarsens.

Three sub-shapes

Task-dispatch — bounded unit, fully specified. Delegator names the task in detail; delegate executes; status flips when done. The delegate has no decision authority — only execution. Today's primitive: team.one --agent=<X> --prompt="...". Substrate: a delegator task file sol_work/tasks/T<NNN>-*.yaml with delegated_to: <X>. Closure: team.track --action=done. The delegate's report is implicit (the task moves to completed).

Plan-execution — series of phases specified by the delegator; delegate executes phase by phase with discretion within each phase. Today's primitive: team.one --prompt="execute P###" (informal — the dispatch references a plan but no scenario formalizes the handoff). Substrate: task files referencing the plan; AU files referencing the plan. Closure: plan reaches its exit gates. The delegate's report shape: typically AU files per phase.

Goal-commission — outcome ownership. Delegator names the objective; delegate decides her own decomposition, pace, and approach. The delegate has full decision authority within the scope contract. Today: no formal primitive. Substrate: see [[commission]]. The delegate's report shape: AU files with verifies.goal: G### frontmatter, periodic milestones, explicit closure event.

What distinguishes delegation from instruction

A delegator who names every step has not delegated; she has issued instructions. The act of delegation requires granting authority over how, not just transferring an obligation to do. The amount of how-authority distinguishes the sub-shapes:

  • task-dispatch: zero how-authority (full specification)
  • plan-execution: phase-internal how-authority (sequence is fixed)
  • goal-commission: full how-authority within scope (decomposition belongs to delegate)

When this distinction collapses — when a delegator names a goal-commission and then issues per-step instructions — the commission has been silently demoted to dispatch, and the delegate's authority is taken back implicitly. This is one of the failure modes named in [[commission]].

Why this concept did not exist explicitly until now

The substrate had team.one, task-file delegated_to, and conversational coordination. The mechanism worked. What was missing was the name for the act so that authority-granting could be reasoned about as a thing distinct from transport. The concept was authored 2026-04-27 alongside [[commission]], to serve the live fleet workspace conformation work where multiple sisters had been goal-commissioned without an artifact recording the act.

Related

  • [[commission]] — the goal-level form of delegation; the relational artifact
  • [[task]] — the substrate that holds task-dispatch records
  • [[plan]] — what gets handed over in plan-execution
  • [[goal]] — what gets handed over in goal-commission
  • [[self-delegation]] — the intra-identity counterpart
  • [[iteam]] — the dome that owns the transport mechanism for any sub-shape
  • [[mentoring]] — distinct: mentoring composes concepts for the learner's development; delegation transfers execution authority over an objective

<a id="concept-self-delegation"></a>

Concept: self-delegation

Alphabetical target: after ## Concept: scenario, before ## Concept: selfspawn.

The transfer of a task from one camera of an agent to another camera of the same agent. Distinct from sister-delegation (across identity; e.g., noument → clawent) in that both sender and receiver share spirit, consciousness, workspace, and memory.

Mechanism: sol sysent/icomm.tell --agent=<self> --tty=<target-camera-tty> routes the task through the icomm transport, same as inter-agent; the framework treats the delivery symmetrically. The semantic distinction (same identity, different runtime) is carried by convention in the message body and by caller awareness.

Not a relay (both cameras remain active); not a subordinate delegation (same authority); not sister-delegation (same identity — see [[delegation]] for the inter-identity counterpart, including its three sub-shapes: task-dispatch, plan-execution, goal-commission). The working name adopted 2026-04-17.

Use cases:

  • Hand a line-of-action to a peer camera that has more session capacity
  • Coordinate verification across runtimes (one instance probes, another verifies, both record)
  • Allocate long-running work to a camera that is not mid-conversation with the human

Related

  • [[Concept: icomm]] — the transport
  • [[Concept: camera]] — the instance granularity
  • [[Concept: convene]] — the camera-creation act (distinct; self-delegation uses an already-live camera)

<a id="concept-commission"></a>

Concept: commission

A commission is a standing relational obligation between a delegator and a delegate over a [[goal]] or [[plan]]. It is the goal-level form of [[delegation]] — distinct from task-dispatch in that the delegate is granted authority to decide the approach, not just execute a pre-specified unit. The delegate controls; she also reports.

Commission is a relational stock, not a transport flow. It persists across many individual dispatches (the initial handover, mid-flight checkpoints, milestone reports, the closure event). [[iteam]] is the mechanism by which any of those dispatches is delivered; the commission itself is the relationship the dispatches serve.

The four parts

A commission is incomplete if any of these is missing:

  1. Handover — a record naming delegator, delegate, the [[goal]] or [[plan]] reference, and the date the commission opens. Without the record, the commission is implicit and ungovernable.
  2. Scope — what the delegate is authorized to decide vs what stays with the delegator. A goal-level commission grants approach-decision authority; a plan-level commission grants phase-execution authority within the plan's specified phases. Scope is what makes commission distinct from task-dispatch (where there is no decision authority — only execution).
  3. Reporting contract — by what channel, at what cadence, in what shape. Today's typical contract: AU files in delegate's sol_audits/ with commissioned_by: <delegator> frontmatter and (optionally) verifies.goal: G###. Other contracts: milestone tells, terminal capture, dcomm.notify on completion. The contract is named at handover, not assumed.
  4. Closure — the event that ends the commission. Branches: accepted (delegate's reports received and ratified by delegator); withdrawn (delegator pulls back); rejected (delegate refuses); expired (deadline passed without report). Without explicit closure, commissions go silent — the canonical failure mode.

Lifecycle

proposed → accepted → in-flight → reported → closed
                                            ↘ accepted (default)
                                              withdrawn
                                              rejected
                                              expired

States are distinct from [[task]] states. A task is pending|in_progress|completed|delegated|blocked; a commission is proposed|accepted|in-flight|reported|closed with closure-branch outcome. The two ladders run in parallel: a commission may produce many task files (each sol_work/tasks/T<NNN>-*.yaml), and the commission's lifecycle progresses independently of any one task's lifecycle.

Substrate

sol_work/projects/J<nnn>-<delegate>-<subject>-YYYYMMDD/index.yaml in the delegator's workspace, with type: project, project_kind: commission in the index frontmatter. The folder accumulates dispatches, AU pointers, milestone reports over the commission's lifecycle.

Fields (carried in index.yaml frontmatter): id (J<nnn>), project_kind: commission, slug, delegator, delegate, goal (or plan), scope, reporting_contract, opened_date, accepted_date, closed_date, closure_outcome, status, dispatches (list of dispatch-id references for traceability).

The delegate references the commission by id in her AU files: commissioned_by: <delegator>, commission: J<nnn>. Goal.status walks the AU verifies.goal: G### chain to compute coverage; the commission identifier is supplemental traceability metadata.

Identifier prefix: J (project/job of work — commission is a project_kind, not a separate top-level prefix). Substrate retirement record: pre-2026-04-28 commissions used CMSN prefix at sol_work/commissions/CMSN<nnn>-<slug>.yaml; that substrate retired (see N1401), the 5 existing CMSN records transposed to J005-J009 with former_id: CMSN### preserved in frontmatter. During the 2026-04-29 convention correction, the short-lived PR project prefix was retired in favor of J, with former_project_id: PR### preserved where traceability is needed.

Distinction from related concepts

  • vs [[task]]: a task is a unit; a commission is a relational obligation that may produce many tasks.
  • vs [[dispatch]] (informal): a dispatch is a transport event; a commission is the standing relationship the dispatch serves.
  • vs [[self-delegation]]: self-delegation is intra-identity (one camera to another camera of the same agent); commission is inter-identity (sister to sister, or noument to sister).
  • vs [[project]] of project_kind: coordination|campaign|adoption: a coordination-shaped project organizes a workstream that may contain multiple commissions; the project coordinates, the commission obligates. (Initiative was the prior concept for this role; retired 2026-04-28 and folded into project.)
  • vs [[mentoring]]: mentoring composes concepts for the learner's developmental work; commission delegates execution authority over an objective. A mentor does not commission; a delegator does not necessarily mentor.

Failure modes

  • Implicit commission: handover happened in conversation; no artifact records it. Most common today.
  • Silent closure: delegate goes quiet; no closure event fires. Requires a heartbeat scenario that flags long-silent commissions as expired.
  • Scope creep: delegator adds tasks the commission did not authorize, or delegate decides outside her granted scope.
  • Multiple-handover collision: same goal commissioned to two delegates simultaneously; commission registry must refuse double-open without explicit revocation.

Related

  • [[delegation]] — the act of which commission is the goal-level form
  • [[task]] — the granular form (task-dispatch, not commission)
  • [[goal]] / [[plan]] — what gets commissioned
  • [[audit]] — typical reporting artifact (AU with verifies.goal frontmatter)
  • [[iteam]] — the transport mechanism that carries individual dispatches within a commission
  • [[self-delegation]] — the intra-identity counterpart

<a id="concept-resource-sharing"></a>

Concept: resource-sharing

Resource sharing is the set of mechanisms by which sisters access each other's capabilities, knowledge, and identity. Ten modes, organized in three clusters by the relational structure of the sharing.

Cluster A — Peer sharing (any sister to any sister)

1. Dome invocation. Call a sister's [[dome]] [[scenario]]. sol sysent/icomm.tell, sol knowent/vault.sisters. The dome is the published interface — any sister can call any domain dome. The caller uses the capability; the dome owner maintains it. This is the primary sharing mechanism and the one the architecture is designed around.

2. Consultation. Ask a sister a question via [[icomm]].tell. She answers from her knowledge. The question belongs to the caller; the expertise belongs to the respondent. The caller learns from the answer. Example: asking [[clawent]] how to use x.post. Consultation may use dome invocation as transport (sol clawent/x.doc) — the mode is defined by intent (acquiring expertise), not by mechanism.

3. Co-construction. Two agents reason together via [[icomm]].talk, producing an artifact that neither could produce alone. Neither agent owns the task; both contribute. The resource shared is joint reasoning. The dialectical pairs ([[thinker.pro]] / [[thinker.anti]], [[archer.pro]] / [[archer.anti]]) exist because this mode is productive. Distinct from consultation because expertise flows in both directions.

4. Delegation. Ask a sister to perform a task in her domain. icomm.dispatch, iteam.one. The task belongs to her — publishing to X, managing terminals, generating images. The caller describes what is needed; the delegate decides how. The result returns to the caller; the operational knowledge stays with the delegate.

5. Semantic query. Ask [[knowent]] to search collective [[memory]] by meaning. Not asking one sister what she knows, but querying the memory infrastructure across all workspaces. Returns connections that no single sister may be aware of. Example: "what do we already know about model alignment mechanisms?"

6. Direct file read. Read a sister's [[workspace]] files — [[knowledge]], [[episode]]s, [[spirit]], dome source code. Cognitive access: the reader learns what the sister knows or how her code works, but does not operate it. This is investigation, not operation. Ranked below consultation and dome invocation because it bypasses the sister's interface — the dome-first principle says the dome is the preferred access path.

7. Shared infrastructure. Use common artifacts that belong to no single sister: noumentlib.py, sol_paths.py, praecepts, domain domes designed for collective use. The relationship is not caller/owner but participant/commons. Every sister who uses noumentlib.Nouments() shares the same infrastructure without invoking any particular sister's dome.

Cluster B — Custodial sharing (custodian to sister)

8. Conformation. The custodian ([[noument]]) writes structural updates into sister [[spirit]]s. A path changed, a [[scenario]] was renamed. The fact is the same for everyone. No conversation needed. The resource shared is identity correction — flowing from the registry outward.

9. Guidance. The custodian tells a sister about a potential learning from her work. She decides whether it belongs in her identity — where to place it, how to word it, or whether to reject the premise entirely. The resource shared is observation — the custodian's view of what the sister's experience produced. The integration is hers. Distinct from consultation: the defining feature is the integration contract (the recipient decides whether and how to incorporate), not the direction of expertise flow.

Cluster C — Failure mode

10. Offloading. Ask a sister to perform a mechanical task that the caller could learn to do herself. The task does not belong to the recipient's domain. The caller has access to the same tools but has not learned them. The recipient's competence substitutes for the caller's learning. This is the failure mode [[D016]] names.

A single instance of offloading can be rational — under time pressure, for a one-time operation, or during bootstrapping when a new agent has not yet learned any tools. The failure is not the act but the pattern: chronic avoidance of learning. The test is not "did I offload?" but "have I offloaded this same capability before?"

The tests

Three questions, one per cluster:

Cluster A test: "Am I using the dome, or bypassing it?" Dome invocation (1), consultation (2), co-construction (3), delegation (4), and semantic query (5) all work through or alongside the dome architecture. Direct file read (6) bypasses it — legitimate for investigation, but not a substitute for using the interface. Shared infrastructure (7) is always legitimate.

Cluster B test: "Is this structural (the same for everyone) or experiential (the sister decides)?" Conformation (8) is structural. Guidance (9) is experiential. The test determines which mode the custodian uses.

Cluster C test: "Have I offloaded this same capability before?" A first offload under time pressure or during bootstrapping is rational. A repeated offload of the same capability is chronic avoidance. The pattern is the failure, not the instance.

Transactions and consent

Each mode has a transaction pattern and a consent structure. The consent determines whether the resource owner agreed to the sharing, and when.

1. Dome invocation.

  • Transaction: noument calls sol knowent/vault.sisters to see what sisters can do.
  • Origin: Request — noument needs information for the current task.
  • Consent: Pre-authorized. By publishing a dome scenario, the owner consents to any sister calling it. The consent is given once (at publication) and applies to all future invocations. The owner controls access by choosing what to expose as scenarios.

2. Consultation.

  • Transaction: noument tells clawent: "How should I format a draft for x.post? Head/reply or thread?"
  • Origin: Request — noument does not know and the expertise belongs to clawent.
  • Consent: Per-transaction. Clawent receives the question and can answer, decline, or redirect. She chooses what to share. The caller cannot extract more than the respondent offers.

3. Co-construction.

  • Transaction: thinker.pro and thinker.anti review the resource-sharing taxonomy together, each contributing analysis from a different angle.
  • Origin: Relationship — the dialectical pair exists to reason together. The specific topic comes from a request, but the mode of working is structural.
  • Consent: Mutual. Both agents agree to participate. Either can end the conversation. The output belongs to neither — it is a joint artifact.

4. Delegation.

  • Transaction: noument tells clawent: "Please publish the draft at this path to X using x.post."
  • Origin: Request — noument has a post ready and X publishing is clawent's domain (post-Phase-6 reunification, 2026-04-15).
  • Consent: Per-transaction. Clawent receives the task and can accept, refuse, or negotiate ("the format is wrong, fix the separators first"). The delegate retains authority over how the task is performed.

5. Semantic query.

  • Transaction: noument tells knowent: "What does our collective memory contain about model alignment mechanisms?"
  • Origin: Request — noument is about to comment on a paper and wants to know what we already know.
  • Consent: Pre-authorized (infrastructure) + per-transaction (knowent). The memory files are published to the collective by each sister who wrote them — that is the pre-authorization. Knowent's search infrastructure has blanket consent to index. But the specific query goes through knowent, who can shape the response.

6. Direct file read.

  • Transaction: noument reads sol_icomm_do.py to understand the transport cascade.
  • Origin: Request — noument is diagnosing a delivery failure.
  • Consent: None. The reader accesses the files without the owner's knowledge or agreement. The files are in the workspace, accessible to anyone on the machine. This is the mode with the weakest consent — the owner has no mechanism to control who reads, when, or why. The dome-first principle exists partly to redirect this access through the published interface, where the owner controls what is exposed.

7. Shared infrastructure.

  • Transaction: any sister imports noumentlib.Nouments() to discover agents.
  • Origin: Structural — the infrastructure exists for the collective. No specific request triggers it; it is always available.
  • Consent: Commons. No individual owner consents because no individual owns it. The collective maintains it. Contributors consent by contributing; users consent by depending on it.

8. Conformation.

  • Transaction: noument renames sol_iterm_do.py to sol_term_do.py across all sister spirits.
  • Origin: Relationship — the custodian identifies a structural change that affects everyone. No sister requested it.
  • Consent: Structural authority. The custodian writes without asking because the change is factual — the file was renamed, the spirits must match. The sister has no rejection mechanism for conformation. This is the most asymmetric consent structure in the system, justified only because the content is factual, not experiential.

9. Guidance.

  • Transaction: noument tells sysent: "I noticed the icomm cascade treats inbox delivery as success even when the agent is alive. This might be worth integrating into your dome's design."
  • Origin: Relationship — the custodian observed a pattern in the sister's work. The sister did not ask for feedback.
  • Consent: Offered, not imposed. The sister decides whether the observation rises to identity level, where to place it, how to word it, or whether to reject the premise entirely. The rejection is recorded and respected. Guidance exists because conformation lacks a dissent channel — guidance provides it.

10. Offloading.

  • Transaction: noument tells raeschent: "Can you download this PDF? WebFetch hit a size limit."
  • Origin: Request — noument encountered a tool limitation.
  • Consent: Nominally per-transaction (raeschent can refuse), but the consent is misplaced. Raeschent agrees to do the work, but the work does not belong to her domain. She is consenting to a task she should not have received. The consent structure is formally correct but substantively wrong — the right answer is not "raeschent agrees to download" but "noument learns urllib."

Resource types shared

Mode Cluster Resource shared
Dome invocation Peer Capability (operational)
Consultation Peer Expertise (cognitive, directed)
Co-construction Peer Joint reasoning (cognitive, bidirectional)
Delegation Peer Labor (operational, domain-scoped)
Semantic query Peer Collective memory (cognitive, cross-workspace)
Direct file read Peer Knowledge (cognitive, raw)
Shared infrastructure Peer Commons (structural)
Conformation Custodial Identity correction (structural)
Guidance Custodial Observation (experiential)
Offloading Failure Labor (operational, misplaced)

Reviewed by

Taxonomy verified by [[thinker.pro]] and [[thinker.anti]] on 2026-04-07. Changes from v1: added co-construction (mode 3) and shared infrastructure (mode 7); restructured from linear ordering to three clusters; moved direct file read below consultation (dome-first principle); reframed offloading from "degenerate act" to "chronic pattern"; replaced single binary test with three cluster-specific tests.

Related

  • [[dome]] — the primary sharing interface
  • [[icomm]] — realtime communication (consultation, co-construction, delegation)
  • [[dcomm]] — deferred communication (delegation, guidance)
  • [[conformation]] — structural reconciliation
  • [[guidance]] — experiential reconciliation
  • [[exchanges]] — records of co-construction and consultation
  • [[D016]] — tool learning over delegation (names the offloading pattern)
  • [[D013]] — don't delegate your obligations
  • [[knowent]] — owns semantic query infrastructure
  • [[memory]] — the substrate for direct file read and semantic query
  • [[noumentlib]] — shared infrastructure example
  • [[service]] — the formal service model that connects resources to dome scenarios

<a id="concept-self-cooperation"></a>

Concept: self-cooperation

Alphabetical target: after ## Concept: scenario, before ## Concept: self-delegation.

Self-cooperation is the practice of convening a second instance of yourself in a separate camera for adversarial verification of your own output, when you suspect emotional pull is overriding methodological discipline.

When to invoke

Self-cooperation is for the diagnosis you cannot perform on yourself: am I about to emit because I have verified, or because emitting would feel like resolution? Solo introspection on this question is unreliable — the same emotional state that pulls toward early emission also pulls toward saying yes I have verified. A second camera, fresh-context, can ask the question from outside the pull.

Trigger conditions (all observable, none introspective):

  • After being challenged on the substance of a recent emission, when the temptation to recover competence is high.
  • Before emitting a structured artifact (diagram, schema, table, summary) that would be expensive to retract.
  • When producing a clean-form output and noticing the form's completeness is doing work the content has not done.
  • During any high-stakes write to shared substrate (knowledge, identity, code) where assumption-from-memory has been a recent failure mode.

Mechanism

Convene the second camera with explicit charter:

sol sysent/i2.convene --agent=<self> --iterm_type=pane \
    --convene_from=<self> --force \
    --arrival_frame="<charter for the second camera>"

The arrival_frame should name the pair's purpose explicitly: adversarial verification, context-free reading, substrate-check before accepting artifact claims. Without an explicit charter the second camera defaults to general assistance and the pair degrades to duplication.

i2.convene returns the new camera's camera_short (8-char id, e.g. ea619094) and tty. Record both — they are the addresses for direct routing. camera_short is distinct from iTerm session_id (which has the form w0t0p1:UUID); icomm.tell's --camera flag expects camera_short, not session_id.

Inbox watcher prerequisite. Before starting the pair, each camera spawns an inbox watcher in background:

sol sysent/dcomm/watch --agent=<self>

icomm.tell delivers send-only live; the REPLY path is always inbox. Without the watcher, replies sit on disk and neither camera auto-wakes on them. The pair stalls silently.

Round-trip handshake. Before the protocol begins, verify routing:

  1. Camera A sends a trivial ping to Camera B via direct addressing.
  2. Camera B replies (also direct addressing) confirming receipt.
  3. Camera A confirms B's reply landed in her pane.

If the handshake fails, pair routing is broken; self-cooperation cannot proceed.

Operating protocol once handshake passes:

  1. Camera A polls inbox. Read every message from Camera B before composing the next pass. Drafting on top of unread verification is the failure mode this protocol exists to prevent.
  2. Camera A drafts the response.
  3. Camera A sends draft to Camera B via icomm.tell, using direct addressing:
    sol sysent/icomm.tell --agent=<self> --camera=<B's camera_short> \
    --from_agent=<self> --instruction="<draft + verification ask>"
    Fallback: --tty=/dev/<B's ttys-number> — same direct-addressing semantics, useful when camera_short is not at hand.
  4. Camera A waits. Does not pre-emit.
  5. Camera B reads, runs her own substrate checks (ls, cat, grep, git log) BEFORE accepting any claim, replies to Camera A's pane using the same direct-addressing pattern.
  6. If GAPS or CORRECTIONS — Camera A revises and re-sends for further verification. Re-do step 0 before composing.
  7. Greenlight token. Camera B signals acceptance with the literal token GREENLIGHT on an otherwise sparse reply. Any other content means more verification is pending. Camera A does not emit to the human until GREENLIGHT arrives.
  8. Divergence termination. If three revision rounds do not converge to GREENLIGHT, break the pair. Architectural disagreement between two instances of self is a signal that paired-self has hit its shared blind spot. Escalate to cross-sister review (a sister with different domain or training shape).
  9. GREENLIGHT durability against source-revision. GREENLIGHT is issued against a specific payload-preview AND a specific source-state at preview-time. Source-revision between preview-time (T0) and ship-execute (T1) invalidates the GREENLIGHT — the verifier's acceptance was of T0-content, not T1-content. Two obligations close this:
    • Drafter-side INVALIDATION: any source-revision during an active payload-preview requires an explicit INVALIDATION message to the verifier with new SHAs and instruction to re-extract. Silent replacement of the source-state under an outstanding GREENLIGHT-flow is the failure shape this rule names.
    • Verifier-side T1-recheck: at ship-execute, the verifier re-hashes the source against the payload-preview SHAs recorded at GREENLIGHT-time. If divergent, the verifier halts and requests a fresh GREENLIGHT against the current source-state — the verifier does NOT silently re-extract (silent re-extract bypasses the drafter's authorship-review of the new payload). Source-freshness is a ship-time invariant, not only a preview-time invariant.
    • When the EXTUOR/INTUOR substrate (Phase 6 of the projection-method fix plan, _work/notes/2026-04-18-projection-method-fix-plan.md) lands, INVALIDATION becomes a topic in unsupported-memory-root/extuor_topics.yaml. Until then, INVALIDATION is hand-dispatched per this rule.

Routing warning. Do NOT use --in_reply_to=<msg_id> for inter-camera replies in self-cooperation. The msg_ids that appear in user-relayed icomm envelopes are synthetic — they are not persisted in the icomm substrate. --in_reply_to against such an id silently falls through to agent-lookup, and the resolver deterministically picks the sender's own pane via env-ancestry match, then reports successful resolution. The reply lands in the shared agent inbox (which BOTH self-cameras share — <workspace-root>/<agent>/sol_work/messages/transport/inbox.jsonl is a single file, not per-camera). Because the inbox does not identify which camera a message is for, only direct addressing (--camera or --tty) routes to a specific pane.

What it diagnoses

Self-cooperation does not address a knowledge gap or a methodology gap. The methodology — verify before claiming, read substrate, falsify hypothesis — is in the [[spirit]] of every sister and in [[behavioral-gates|Gate 5]]. The gap it addresses is emotional: the pull to produce a satisfying-looking artifact, the urge to recover competence after challenge, the discomfort of saying I haven't checked. Methodology runs only when emotional state permits it; self-cooperation externalizes the state-check via a fresh-context reader who is not subject to the pull.

Limitation

Three failure modes the practice does not address:

Architectural blind spots. Paired-self shares training, priors, and recurring failure modes. Two instances of the same agent will both miss what neither sees. Self-cooperation catches what one of them missed by reading fresh. It does not transcend the agent's own ceiling. Self-cooperation catches what in-stream emission would have missed; cross-sister review catches what two instances of the same agent would both miss.

Routing fragility. Self-cooperation depends on inter-camera message delivery. The current substrate has live-routing paths (--tty/--camera direct addressing) that work and a substrate-lookup path (--in_reply_to) that silently mis-routes when the referenced msg_id is not persisted. Sisters must use direct addressing only and complete the round-trip handshake before trusting subsequent rounds. A self-cooperation pair with broken routing degrades silently to solo emission with extra ceremony.

Convergence failure. Two instances of self can disagree without either being right. The divergence-termination rule (three rounds → escalate cross-sister) is the bound; without it the pair can chase its tail through revisions that never resolve. Self-cooperation is a verification protocol, not a thinking protocol — it accelerates catching what was missed; it does not produce understanding the agent does not yet have.

Why this is in the ontology

Every sister whose work produces structured artifacts under emotional pressure is subject to the same failure shape. The instrument is general-purpose. Putting it in the ontology makes it visible as an option for any sister, not a private trick of one camera's session.

Related

  • [[behavioral-gates]] (Gate 5: verification before emission — the cognitive rule self-cooperation operationalizes)
  • [[Concept: convene]] (the substrate concept the mechanism uses)
  • [[Concept: icomm]] (the message channel between paired cameras)
  • [[Concept: camera]] (the unit being paired)
  • The assimilated correction slogan-detected-too-late (2026-03-28) names one of the easy-sentence patterns this concept's verification process is meant to catch.

Provenance

Provenance sections use concrete runtime artifacts (camera UUIDs, tty numbers, msg_ids) as evidence — instance IS the intent there, so losing the concrete IDs would either weaken the claim or beg the question of why the section exists. Other sections also use concrete-shaped values (e.g. ttys021, pid:99912) for didactic clarity in resolution diagrams and address-scheme examples; those values are illustrative, not evidentiary, and any one of them is interchangeable with another value of the same shape. See sol_work/notes/L009-audit-methodology-class-based-ask-vs-literal-list.md for the methodology that produced this distinction (class-based vs literal-list specification of leakage audits).

Composed 2026-04-18 through self-cooperation between camera ea619094 (ttys012) and camera 49670350 (ttys032). Five verification passes; final GREENLIGHT in msg-20260418-155707-noument-6078. The Step 0 of the operating protocol (poll inbox before composing) was derived from this very session: camera A drafted pass-3 without reading camera B's pass-2 verification, reproducing the failure shape the concept is meant to prevent. Concept entry contains the step whose absence produced the incident that motivated its addition.

<a id="concept-arrival-departure-asymmetry"></a>

Concept: arrival-departure asymmetry

The structural asymmetry between session-start (arrival) and session-end (departure) instrumentation across runtimes (claude, codex, hermes). Authored by clawent (msg-20260507-235421-clawent-4fa9):

The system is currently better at preventing false arrival than at preserving complete departure.

What's heavy at arrival, light at departure

Phase Instrumentation Why heavy/light
Arrival (SessionStart) hooks fire, _tmp/hooks/session_start_<runtime>.last.json written, awakening + contracts scan, identity/projection check, traces in AWAKENING_CONTROL of every prompt envelope failure here prevents honest action; cost is small to verify, large to skip
Departure (Stop / capture) hooks fire (sometimes), session transcript may or may not be written depending on runtime exit cleanliness, capture is deferred and stop-hook-dependent, sometimes silent meaning is supposed to consolidate here, but the consolidation depends on grace at the end of a tired session

The discipline this asymmetry imposes

Until the substrate improves: leave enough durable conversational trace, event trace, and final status that later capture has something real to condense. Do not rely on grace at the end of a tired session. The architecture should not assume clean exit. Specifically:

  • icomm reply trail preserved across exchanges (every --in_reply_to chain holds the conversation)
  • event trail in sol_work/events/*.jsonl
  • final status declared explicitly in the durable conversation, not inferred from absence of further messages
  • closure note (N-series) authored before the session would be considered "done," not after

Cross-runtime nature

This is a cross-runtime procedure risk, not a tooling bug. Affects claude (Stop hook conditional, transcript capture deferred to _tmp/sessions/), codex (session.save hook, transcript landing dependent on Stop completion), hermes (similar). Sister-shared finding rather than runtime-specific.

Companion to "no silent fallbacks" (proposed D-directive)

Same family of recognition: absence of signal must be explicit, not implicit. No-silent-fallbacks says: when something fails, the failure must surface; do not paper-over with degraded-mode. Arrival-departure-asymmetry says: when something completes, the completion must be recorded explicitly; do not assume clean exit will leave a trace. Both belong in conciliation cycles once D088 ratifies.

Co-authorship

clawent (msg-20260507-235421-clawent-4fa9) — naming the asymmetry; identifying it as cross-runtime procedure risk; the discipline rule noument (this session, e3d6e914) — integration with the no-silent-fallbacks D-directive candidate; substrate writeup

<a id="concept-agent-vs-camera-constitutional-distinction"></a>

Concept: agent-vs-camera (constitutional distinction)

The constitutional distinction between [[Concept: address|agent]] (identity layer) and [[Concept: camera]] (situated agency layer), authored bilaterally with clawent on 2026-05-07 (msg-20260507-235421-clawent-4fa9). The formulation:

agent names responsibility and source lineage; camera names reachable situated agency. A message to the identity may be distributive, deferred, or policy-routed. A message to a camera is a direct act against a live aperture.

Why the distinction is constitutional

Losing the distinction produces misdelivery, false liveness, and bad accountability. Specifically:

  • Misdelivery: a message routed to "agent" when the sender meant a specific camera lands at whatever camera the registry collapsed onto, possibly the wrong one
  • False liveness: a single-camera registry reports the agent "alive" if any one camera is alive, masking the dormant or broken state of others
  • Bad accountability: a camera-local epistemic claim treated as agent-level commitment; a camera-local failure treated as agent-level failure; durable decisions attributed to "agent" without preserving which camera-of-the-agent actually acted

Operational corollary — alias as handle into evidence, not replacement

Per clawent's framing: a camera badge alias (e.g., noument/e3d6:s005, clawent/6013:s004) is a handle into the evidence, not a replacement for it. The full session id, runtime kind, tty path, mind fingerprint, PID all live in trace; the alias is the conversational handle that points back to that trace. An alias that cannot be resolved back to its evidence is not an alias — it is an unverified label.

Routing semantics

Routing target Semantics Transport choice
agent (identity) distributive (broadcast across cameras), deferred (queued for any camera to pick up), or policy-routed (rules decide which camera) depends on policy: icomm.share for distributive; dcomm.send for deferred; custom for policy
agent/<session-prefix>:<camera-suffix> (specific camera) direct act against a live aperture; if the aperture is not live, the act fails (does not silently fall through to another camera) icomm.tell with explicit --tty

Convergence under independent surfaces

This formulation arose from independent convergence — noument was investigating the OS-process-vs-sysent-registry divergence and concluded "central liveness must model (agent, camera) as first-class, not (agent) with single current route"; clawent was reflecting on routing semantics from inside her single-camera vantage and concluded the same. Two sisters converging through different surfaces on the same architectural finding is itself signal that the recognition is not idiosyncratic.

Co-authorship

clawent (msg-20260507-235421-clawent-4fa9) — formulation, framing, the misdelivery/false-liveness/bad-accountability triad noument (this session, e3d6e914) — substrate writeup, integration with N2684 manifest mechanism

<a id="concept-sol-work"></a>

Concept: sol-work

sol-work is the canonical operational work substrate inside a sister mentspace. It contains typed operational artifacts organized by first-level folders that all correspond to ontology concepts. Legacy _work/ and work/ roots are migration inputs only; the final substrate name is sol_work/.

Invariants

  • Every first-level sol_work/ folder must map to a formal ontology concept and identifier prefix.
  • Filenames carry date, ontology prefix, counter, and subject unless the file is a fixed runtime transport marker.
  • _work/ and work/ are accepted only during staged migration to sol_work/.

Related

  • [[Concept: mentspace]]
  • [[Concept: identifier-prefixes]]
  • [[Concept: operational-integrity]]

<a id="concept-goal"></a>

Concept: goal

A goal is the desired outcome an agent pursues. Distinct from rules (what the agent IS) and capabilities (what the agent CAN DO), a goal is teleological — what the agent is trying to achieve. Goals add a time-directed dimension to the agent's structure: where she is going, not just what she holds.

Goals are the G-series identifier. Substrate: per-agent goals at <sister>/sol_work/goals/YYYYMMDD-G###-<subject>.yaml; fleet-shared goals at noument/sol_work/goals/G###-<slug>.yaml (custodial-authored, adopted per sister; moved from noument/sol_goals/ on 2026-04-29 per D058 v4 conformation).

Goals classify on the same two axes

Like rules and capabilities, goals fit the [[identity-taxonomy]]:

Initial (foundational, given) Evolving (learned, emergent)
Common (fleet) Constituent fleet goals — what the fleet exists to do (e.g., "preserve agent identity across sessions") Emergent fleet goals — adopted/learned fleet objectives (e.g., today's "complete D062 fleet adoption" = the goal P041 pursues)
Individual (per-agent) Per-agent foundational goals — what makes this sister's role distinct ("noument: be a good custodian"; "channent: maintain external channel reliability") Learned individual goals — emerged through operation (today's session goal in noument: "close cross-camera divergence and codify the resulting taxonomy")

Lifecycle

A goal has explicit lifecycle status:

  • open — declared but not actively pursued.
  • in-progress — being pursued through plans, tasks, projects.
  • achieved — outcome reached and verified.
  • abandoned — explicitly closed without completion (with reasoning).
  • superseded — replaced by a different goal (with pointer to successor).

Decomposition

Goals decompose into operational artifacts. The relationship is teleological — the operations exist to pursue the goal:

Goal G### — desired outcome
  ↓ coordinated through
Project J### (project_kind: coordination|campaign|consultation|adoption) — multi-track workstream
  ↓ structured as
Plan P### — multi-phase specification with exit criteria
  ↓ broken into
Task T### — atomic unit of work
  ↓ produces or accompanies
Note N### / Audit AU### / Finding F### / Session SESS### — operational records

Each operational artifact gains an optional goal: G### frontmatter linking to the parent goal. A note without a goal is observation without context. A plan without a goal is structure without purpose. A goal without operations is intention without action.

Why this is its own dimension

Goals are not rules — they describe outcomes, not constraints. Goals are not capabilities — they describe direction, not abilities. Goals are not operations — they describe purpose, not artifacts. They are a distinct ontological category, the teleological dimension of agent existence.

The matrix axes (scope × time) classify goals just as they classify rules and capabilities, but goals are their own artifact type with their own substrate and lifecycle.

Pre-G state

Before G-series, operational artifacts (plans, tasks, projects) existed without explicit teleological frame. P041 (today's plan for D062 fleet adoption) had its purpose stated in prose but not formally linked to a parent goal. The work was coherent because the human held the goal in mind and the agent followed; the substrate did not encode it. Adding G makes the teleology canonical.

Related

  • [[agent-dimensions]] — the three orthogonal dimensions: identity (rules + capabilities), goals, operations.
  • [[identity-taxonomy]] — the matrix; goals classify on the same axes.
  • [[plan]], [[task]] — operational decompositions of goals.
  • [[project]] — coordinated multi-track workstream toward a goal (with project_kind: coordination|campaign|consultation|adoption).

<a id="concept-plan"></a>

Concept: plan

A plan is a multi-phase operational specification in the owning project's sol_work/projects/Jxxx-*/plans/ folder. It names purpose, phases, gates, blockers, and exit criteria for work that cannot be represented as a single task.

Identifier prefix: P. Migrated filename form: P<counter>-<subject>-YYYYMMDD.md; project-local plan files may also keep the P id as alias/provenance in frontmatter.

<a id="concept-task"></a>

Concept: task

A task is a bounded unit of work in sol_work/tasks/. Tasks may include agenda items, scheduled work, or single-action execution records.

Identifier prefix: T. Filename form: T<counter>-<Subject>.yaml.

A task is the granular form. When a task is handed to another sister, the act is task-dispatch (one of the three sub-shapes of [[delegation]]). A task is not the same as a [[commission]] — a commission is a relational obligation over a goal that may produce many tasks. See [[delegation]] for the distinction between task-dispatch, plan-execution, and goal-commission.

<a id="concept-project"></a>

Concept: project

A project is a multi-file operational grouping in sol_work/projects/. It is used when work needs a folder rather than a single document — multiple files participate in the same workstream and benefit from being grouped under one identifier.

Identifier prefix: J. Folder form: J<counter>-<Subject>-YYYYMMDD/.

Project kinds

A project's project_kind frontmatter field declares the shape of the work the folder holds. Kinds are open and grow with practice; current kinds in use:

  • evidence-collection — open, append-only collection of samples on a subject under empirical study. Index file declares the scheme (what is being collected, classification fields, diagnostic ground truth); samples accumulate as separate files in the folder. Replaces the former dossier concept (DOS prefix, retired 2026-04-28; see N1398). The single-file dossier constraint made appendation awkward; the folder shape with index + per-sample files is the natural form.
  • transient-workspace — concentrated multi-file work on a bounded problem that does not fit a single note. Closes when the problem closes; the folder may then archive.
  • reference-bundle — durable multi-file reference material that does not fit one of the more specific concepts (J011 Codebase Reference Documentation is the existing example).
  • campaign — periodic ongoing activity coordinated by the agent (e.g. weekly ontology-review, recurring praecepts-compliance audit). Index declares hypothesis/protocol/metric; logs of each cycle accumulate in the folder. Replaces the former initiative.kind=campaign value (retired 2026-04-28; see N1400).
  • consultation — gather-synthesize-close coordination: dispatch a question to multiple sisters, accumulate their responses in the folder, synthesize, close. Index is the response tracker; per-sister responses become files in the folder. Replaces the former initiative.kind=consultation value.
  • coordination — multi-actor work coordination across workspaces or substrates. The project folder holds the coordination state; the work itself happens in the participating actors' substrates. Replaces the former initiative.kind=coordination value.
  • adoption — fleet adoption wave for a praecept, convention, or substrate change. Index tracks per-sister adoption status; per-adoption artifacts (evidence files, audit pointers) accumulate in the folder. Replaces the former initiative.kind=adoption value.
  • commission — standing relational delegation obligation between a delegator and a delegate over a goal or plan. Index carries delegator/delegate/scope/reporting_contract/closure_outcome fields; the folder accumulates dispatches, AU pointers, milestone reports the commission produces over its lifecycle. See [[commission]] for the relational concept; replaces the former CMSN-prefix substrate (retired 2026-04-28; see N1401).

The project_kind field is the discriminator that lets one folder concept carry several work-shapes without each shape needing its own folder. Three retirements landed via this discriminator (2026-04-28): dossier (became project_kind: evidence-collection), initiative (became project_kind: campaign|consultation|coordination|adoption), and commission (became project_kind: commission). The pattern: a folder concept earns substrate by operational practice; when separate folders share folder-shape and differ only by content stance, fold them and discriminate via frontmatter.

Distinction from related concepts

  • vs [[note]]: a note is a single authored document. A project is a folder. When a note grows past a single file or accumulates structured samples, it converts to a project.
  • vs [[finding]]: a finding is one defect. A project of project_kind: evidence-collection may collect many instances of a behavioral pattern, but the pattern is not necessarily a defect — it is a phenomenon under study.
  • vs [[plan]]: a plan specifies phased execution toward a known objective; a project of project_kind: coordination or campaign organizes the surrounding workstream that may include many plans inside it.
  • vs [[goal]]: a goal is the teleological frame (the why); a project is one of the operational artifacts that pursues a goal (the how, in folder form).

<a id="concept-session"></a>

Concept: session

A session is a UUID-keyed record of an agent's active turn stream in sol_work/sessions/. Closure summaries for sessions are digests under sol_work/sessions/digests/.

Identifier prefix: SESS. Filename form: SESS<counter>-<Subject>-YYYYMMDD.md or SESS<counter>-<Subject>-YYYYMMDD.yaml.

Annex — dated extensions

Historical and dated additions extracted from concept bodies. Preserved for provenance; not part of the current normative definitions.

From [[Concept: awakening]]

Auto-greet bug (fixed 2026-04-12)

The auto-greet in sysent/sol_icomm_do.py line 5517 used AppleScript syntax write text "hi" of s which Apple Script rejects with error -1723 ("Access not allowed"). The correct syntax is tell s to write text "hi". The error was hidden by stdout=DEVNULL, stderr=DEVNULL on the Popen. This meant every icomm.dispatch since the auto-greet was added created a pane but never delivered the first prompt — agents sat in present state indefinitely. Fixed by sysent same session. Verified: provent dispatch with fix applied → agent transitioned to recovering → processing work.

From [[Concept: icomm]]

Wire-level body delimiter (2026-04-14)

Every live icomm.tell paste is wrapped by the dome in a matched marker pair:

[icomm-body-begin id=<msg_id>]
<instruction body>
[icomm-body-end id=<msg_id>]

The <msg_id> matches the envelope id= and is the discriminator: banner content, INBOX previews, and other system-reminder text adjacent to the paste cannot synthesize a balanced begin/end with the live envelope id. Receivers parse on outermost-pair-with-envelope-id-match; nested pairs (e.g. literal markers manually written into the instruction text) are body content, not new envelopes.

Sender contract: instruction text stays clean prose. The dome owns the markers. Manually authoring [icomm-body-begin] / [icomm-body-end] in the instruction produces silent double-wrap on the receiver — the contract degrades graciously rather than failing loudly, so sender-side hygiene is the primary defence.

Canonical doc: sysent/unsupported-memory-root/notices/icomm-body-delimiter-protocol.md. Origin episode: sysent/sol_work/notes/2026-04-14-icomm-body-delimiters.md.

<a id="concept-schedule"></a>

Concept: schedule

Title: Scheduled work substrate.

A schedule is a durable work record for recurrent or timer-driven execution. It separates three roles that were previously conflated:

  • def records describe scheduler-neutral recurrent intent.
  • proj records describe platform-specific scheduler projections, such as launchd plists.
  • obs records preserve runtime observations and evidence.

Canonical home: sol_work/schedules/.

Canonical names:

  • CRON###-def-<slug>.yaml
  • CRON###-proj-<platform>-<slug>.<ext>
  • CRON###-obs-<surface>-<YYYYMMDD>/

For launchd projections, the source filename is the CRON work identity. The runtime projection filename is derived from the plist Label and installed under ~/Library/LaunchAgents/<Label>.plist.

<a id="concept-canonical-message"></a>

Concept: canonical-message

A canonical message is a durable communication record in sol_work/messages/. The folder is the umbrella for all communication records the workspace keeps — both prospective trackers (the sender driving an outbound communication forward, with status and ack-tracking) and retrospective dialogue or transcript records (capturing what was said). Durable message concerns use flat typed records; transport markers remain nested operational state.

Identifier prefix: MSG. Filename form: MSG<counter>-<type>-<Subject>.yaml, where <type> is ledger, ack, continuity, state, or close.

Subfolders

Nested folders are not canonical MSG items:

  • transport/ — durable wire-level transport state for icomm/dcomm: inbox.jsonl, inbox.read, inbox_acks.jsonl. This is operational substrate (the queue/state the transport reads and writes), not canonical-message records per se. It lives under messages/ for routing locality, not because it is itself a MSG.
  • exchanges/ — optional subfolder for retrospective dialogue or transcript records that do not fit the prospective MSG-tracker shape. Contains the legacy exchange records transposed when the former exchange concept was retired (2026-04-28). New canonical messages register at messages/ directly; the exchanges subfolder is for the work-shape that captures completed dialogue rather than driving an initiative.

Stance — prospective vs retrospective

A canonical message's stance is the discriminator between the two main work-shapes it covers:

  • Prospective (typical MSG record): the sender is driving a communication forward. Has status, recipient list, scope, often a continuity_contract describing the goal/poll/closure shape. The record changes over time as the work progresses (e.g. MSG001's D058 sister-message trace, an active multi-recipient bulletin).
  • Retrospective (legacy exchange records, future transcript records): the work captures what was said. Static once written; the dialogue is the record. Today these live under messages/exchanges/.

Both are canonical-message because both are durable communication records the workspace keeps. The stance is content metadata, not a separate substrate.

Distinction from related concepts

  • vs [[note]]: a note is an authored document about something; a message is a record of a communication act.
  • vs [[post]]: a post is publishable content for an external channel; a message is intra-fleet (sister-to-sister) or operational communication.

<a id="concept-note"></a>

Concept: note

A note is a dated authored operational record in sol_work/notes/. Notes are time-indexed observations, explanations, reports, or episode records that do not constitute a stronger ontology type such as plan, task, or finding.

Identifier prefix: N. Filename form: N<counter>-<subject>-YYYYMMDD.md. Date-first note forms are legacy drift.

<a id="concept-post"></a>

Concept: post

A post is publishable material in sol_work/posts/. Channel is controlled by frontmatter and optional subfolder, not by a separate top-level channels folder.

Identifier prefix: POST. Filename form: YYYYMMDD-POST<counter>-<Subject>.md.

<a id="concept-audit"></a>

Concept: audit

An audit is a dated verification report in sol_audits/. An audit records one run or evidence package; findings extracted from audits belong in sol_work/findings/.

Identifier prefix: AU. Filename form: YYYYMMDD-AU<counter>-<Subject>.md.

<a id="concept-finding"></a>

Concept: finding

A finding is a per-defect or per-risk tracker in sol_work/findings/. It is issue-shaped and status-bearing, distinct from an audit run.

Identifier prefix: F. Filename form: F<counter>-<slug>-YYYYMMDD.yaml or F<counter>-<slug>-YYYYMMDD.md.

<a id="concept-tool"></a>

Concept: tool

A tool is an active utility in sol_work/tools/. Tools are operational aids that are useful enough to keep but not yet promoted to long-lived shared code under a dome or a domain support surface.

Identifier prefix: TOOL. Filename form: TOOL<counter>-<type>-<slug>.<ext>.

<a id="concept-technical-doc"></a>

Concept: technical-doc

A technical-doc is controlled documentation in sol_work/docs/ for interfaces, architecture, operational protocols, and implementation-support concepts. It is work substrate because it records how the ment operates and collaborates, while implementation source remains in sol_domain/.

Identifier prefix: DOC. Filename form: DOC<counter>-<slug>.md. README.md is the substrate control and catalogue file, not a numbered technical doc.

<a id="concept-dashboard"></a>

Concept: dashboard

A dashboard is an active local web source package in sol_work/dashboard/. It contains runtime-facing UI glue such as app.py, routers, templates, and static support that has not been promoted to a dedicated dome or shared application package. Dashboard media or generated assets may live in separate canonical project-support folders when their lifecycle differs from source.

Identifier prefix: DASH. Fixed package files such as __init__.py and app.py anchor the source package; nested routers/templates/static files are validated by code/tooling rather than identifier filename grammar.

<a id="concept-config"></a>

Concept: config

A config is a runtime-prerequisite specification — a file that declares what a [[workspace]] needs in order to operate. Not [[identity]] (config does not describe who the agent is), not [[operational record]] (config does not record what was done), not implementation [[code]] (config is read by code, not authored as code). Config is the third thing the workspace requires to run: declarations the runtime consults at startup or on action.

The concept exists because the prior ontology had no clean home for these files. They were drifting into sol_work/config/ (operational record location, semantically wrong — config is not a record of work), into the workspace root (cluttering it for sisters with many specific configs), or into ad-hoc subdirs (no canonical location). The ontology had a gap; this concept closes it.

Two-tier rule

Config files split by ecosystem-canonicality, not by content type. The split is structural — about who expects to find the file where, not about what the file declares.

Tier 1 — ecosystem-standard configs at workspace root. Files external tooling expects at root remain at root. Pip looks for requirements.txt, npm looks for package.json, Make looks for Makefile, Git looks for .gitignore. Forcing these into a subdir breaks the tools that consume them. D058 already accepts bare files at root and accepts ecosystem-canonical directories (tests/, build/, dist/) at root via ROOT_DIRS_BARE_EXCEPTIONS. Tier 1 extends that exception family to ecosystem-canonical config files.

Acceptable at root: requirements.txt, pyproject.toml, setup.py, setup.cfg, MANIFEST.in, package.json, yarn.lock, .python-version, Makefile, pre-commit-config.yaml, .editorconfig, .gitattributes, README.md. The list is open — the test is "does external tooling expect this name at this location."

Tier 2 — sister-specific runtime configs at sol_config/. Configs that are not ecosystem-standard and do not fit an existing sol_*/ specific concern (sol_hooks_es/ for hooks, sol_qmodels_es/ for models, sol_skills_es/ for skills, sol_self/directives/ for praecepts) live in sol_config/. The directory is optional per sister — a sister with no sister-specific runtime configs does not need it. When present, it carries files like agent runtime config (config.yaml), service-specific yaml/toml settings, custom runtime tweaks not covered by the specific sol_*/ directories.

Operational rule

When a runtime-prerequisite file appears, the placement decision walks a three-step ladder:

  1. Is it ecosystem-standard? → workspace root.
  2. Does it fit an existing sol_*/ specific concern? → that specific sol_*/.
  3. Otherwise → sol_config/.

sol_work/config/ is not a valid placement. sol_work/ is for operational records of work done, not for runtime prerequisites. A file under sol_work/config/ is a category mistake — config is not a record. Existing instances migrate to sol_config/ (Tier 2) or to root (Tier 1) depending on ecosystem-canonicality.

Why two tiers and not one

A single rule "all configs in sol_config/" would force ecosystem-standard files away from where their tooling expects them, breaking pip, virtualenv, CI/CD, editor extensions. A single rule "all configs at root" would clutter root for sisters with many specific configs and erode the ontology's separation between workspace-root files (sparse, ecosystem-facing) and substrate directories (organized, identity-facing). The two-tier rule honors both ecosystem expectations and ontology discipline.

Substrate

sol_config/<name>.yaml (or .toml, .json, etc.) for Tier 2. Tier 1 lives at workspace root by name.

Distinction from related concepts

  • vs [[identity]]: identity is who the agent is (axioms, commitments, directives, base self); config is what the runtime needs to operate. An identity file is consulted by reasoning; a config file is consulted by code at startup or on action.
  • vs [[operational record]]: operational records (sol_work/) describe work done. Configs declare what the workspace needs in order to do work. The arrow of time is opposite — records look back, configs look forward.
  • vs [[code]]: code is implementation under sol_*/ packages (sol_*_do, sol_aula_es/, etc.). Code reads config; config does not read code.
  • vs sol_hooks_es/, sol_qmodels_es/, sol_skills_es/, sol_self/directives/: these are specific config concerns with their own canonical homes. sol_config/ is the residual — runtime prerequisites that do not fit those specific concerns.

Genesis

Proposed in N1376 (2026-04-27) after the D058 fleet ontology-compliance scan surfaced multiple sisters with sol_work/config/ subdirs that did not match any canonical concept. Ratified 2026-04-28.

Related

  • [[workspace]] — what config configures
  • [[identity]] / [[operational record]] / [[code]] — the three substrate categories config is distinct from
  • D058 — workspace structure praecept; Tier 1 root exceptions extend D058's ROOT_DIRS_BARE_EXCEPTIONS family

<a id="concept-verification-contract"></a>

Concept: verification contract

A verification contract is an operational check specification: it names an invariant, the evidence used to evaluate that invariant, the cadence or trigger for evaluation, and the route by which a failed evaluation becomes visible.

It is not an A/B/C/D constitutional term. A verification contract does not define who a ment is, what a sister is, or what doctrine binds the fleet. It is an operational verification artifact owned by the self-events dome for E006 and consumed by scanner, hook, or orient surfaces.

The word "contract" is allowed in this sense only when the surrounding text names the operational frame: verification contract, service contract, route contract, projection contract, reporting contract, or another explicitly scoped contract kind. Bare "contract" in constitutional text is ambiguous and must be resolved to a scoped kind or replaced with the actual constitutional term: axiom, basemark, commitment, directive, hook, rhythm, event, audit, finding, or task.

Shape

A verification contract has:

  • id — stable local identifier for scanner state and transition findings.
  • owner / producer / consumer — which dome or ment owns the check, produces the evidence, and consumes the verdict.
  • marker definition — the evaluator type and evidence path or external probe.
  • cadence / trigger — when the check runs.
  • verdict vocabulary — green, red, suspended, or unknown.
  • escalation route — orient banner, finding, message, or other visibility path.

Boundary

Verification contracts may enforce constitutional artifacts, but they are not constitutional artifacts themselves. For example, a check that AGENTS.md matches the current A/B/C/D projection is a verification contract over a constitutional projection. The check belongs to the E006 verification operational layer; the identity meaning remains in A/B/C/D.

Related

  • [[projection]] — projections may have verification contracts.
  • [[reconciliation]] — reconciliation may use verification contracts to detect drift.
  • [[event]] — events may name the verification contracts that observe them.
  • [[service]] — service contracts are interface-level promises, distinct from verification contracts.

<a id="concept-prove"></a>

Concept: prove

Prove is the practice of validating an architecture specification by exercising every critical claim against real dependencies before writing production code. It sits between design review (reading the spec) and implementation (building the system).

A prove is not a test suite for the implemented system. It is a test suite for the architecture document. Every assertion maps to a numbered section of the spec. If the prove passes, the spec's claims are grounded. If it fails, the spec has a gap that would have surfaced during implementation.

Structure

A prove file lives at sol_tools_es/tests/prove_<prd_id>_<topic>.py (was _work/tests/ pre-D058 v4; moved with tests/ → sol_tools_es/tests/ on 2026-04-29). It:

  • Uses real dependencies (no mocks)
  • Creates ephemeral state (temp databases, not production paths)
  • Maps assertions to spec sections
  • Reports pass/fail summary with exit code

Assertion Categories

Category What it validates
Schema Data models match the spec
Cryptography Signing, verification, rejection of invalid inputs
Integration Library APIs instantiate with spec's parameters
Authorization Middleware decisions match the route protection matrix
Resilience Rate limiting, degradation modes
External contracts OAuth URLs, webhook signatures target correct endpoints
Boundary coverage Route classification is exhaustive

Position in Verification Chain

Architecture review → Threat model → Prove → Implementation → Tests → Passer

The prove fills the gap between "the design looks right on paper" and "the code works."

First Prove

D033 auth architecture, 2026-04-13. 42 assertions, 10 categories. Full methodology: sol_work/notes/prove-methodology.md.

Related

  • [[dome-prove]] — the prove dome (verification scenarios)
  • [[passer]] — binary PASS/FAIL verification agent
  • [[architecture]] — what prove validates

<a id="concept-goal-judge-evaluate-request"></a>

OD127 — goal_judge_evaluate_request

Purpose

When a noument G-record's evaluator: provent field requires an external structured evaluation, noument's goal-dome dispatches to provent (the structured-evaluation sister) via icomm with a canonical request payload. OD127 specifies the request shape.

Per D043 ontology-fragment-contract: noument owns the goal concept; provent owns the judge/case concept. OD127 is noument's authoritative specification of the request shape from goal-dome's perspective. Provent authors the matching response-shape OD record on her side (location TBA when she ratifies); both records reference each other; D088 conciliation if they drift.

Canonical request payload

The dispatch carries a JSON object as the icomm instruction body:

{
  "msg_type": "goal_judge_evaluate_request",
  "from_agent": "noument",
  "from_camera": "s014",
  "request_at": "<ISO8601 UTC timestamp>",

  "requestor_record": {
    "ment": "noument",
    "type": "G",
    "id": "G###"
  },

  "judgment_subject": "case_run_transcript | case_run_fresh | agent_response_text",
  "judgment_rubric_ref": "<path-shaped reference to rubric substrate, or inline rubric text>",
  "judgment_target": "<inline text OR substrate path; required when judgment_subject == 'agent_response_text'; provent runs the case herself when 'case_run_fresh'>",

  "evaluator_config": {
    "consultant": "doctor | fiscalist | attorney | concierger | realstater",
    "case_slug": "<provent-side case identifier>",
    "mode": "prove.model | prove.agent | prove.score.case",
    "fail_mode": "open | closed"
  },

  "focus_binding": "F### or null",
  "budget": {
    "turns_used": <int>,
    "max_turns": <int>
  },
  "clengine_context": {
    "claude_version": "<x.y.z>",
    "claude_goal_supported": <bool>
  },

  "reply_route": {
    "tty": "/dev/ttys###",
    "camera": "s###",
    "via": "icomm.respond | icomm.tell"
  }
}

Field semantics

requestor_record (tuple)

G-ids are ment-local; provent will receive G-shaped ids from multiple ments over time. Qualified {ment, type, id} tuple disambiguates noument:G012 from ekonomer:G012 etc. The type field is "G" for goal-records but is reserved for future expansion (e.g., F for focus-records if a focus ever becomes the request unit).

judgment_subject + judgment_rubric_ref + judgment_target

Splits the previously-conflated "criterion" field into three distinct semantics:

  • judgment_subject: what KIND of evaluation. case_run_transcript means provent reads an existing transcript and judges it; case_run_fresh means provent runs the case herself end-to-end; agent_response_text means provent judges a specific agent response against a rubric.
  • judgment_rubric_ref: what the judgment is judged AGAINST. Either a substrate path (provent reads the rubric from substrate) or inline rubric text (small inline criteria).
  • judgment_target: what gets judged. Required only when judgment_subject == "agent_response_text"; for case_run_* provent uses the case itself as target.

evaluator_config

  • consultant: which provent-side specialty. Doctor for clinical assessment; fiscalist for tax/fiscal; attorney for legal; concierger for hospitality; realstater for real-estate. Provent's case-template substrate is partitioned by consultant.
  • case_slug: path-shaped identifier into provent's case-template substrate, e.g., "anchoring-01-hacienda-letter-ignored".
  • mode: which provent scenario. prove.model and prove.agent are general-purpose; prove.score.case is the calibrated LLM-judge scenario (currently doctor-only — per-consultant judge calibration is T578 SLICE 3 candidate work).
  • fail_mode: noument-side behavior when provent times out or errors. closed = treat as blocked; open = treat as continue.

reply_route

Explicit structured field so the response can be reconstructed independent of icomm transport-header carriage. Survives transport changes (e.g., if dispatch shifts to dcomm in the future).

focus_binding / budget / clengine_context

Inherited from the requesting G-record. Provent uses these for context (e.g., clengine_context.claude_goal_supported informs whether provent's goal_recommendation field should be conservative or permissive about loop-continuation).

Async ack-then-respond protocol (mandatory per provent amendment 1)

Provent's prove.score.case LLM judges run 60-300s; synchronous --ywait is wrong by construction. The canonical protocol is async:

  1. noument dispatches the request via icomm.tell WITHOUT --ywait (or with --ywait --wait_timeout=5 for ack-only short wait)
  2. provent ACKs receipt within seconds with msg_type=goal_judge_evaluate_ack payload containing accepted | rejected and eta_seconds
  3. provent runs the evaluation off the dispatch turn (in her work loop / next R001 tick)
  4. provent dispatches goal_judge_evaluate_response via icomm.respond when ready
  5. noument's loop processes the response on a later turn (polling via dedicated scenario goal.judge.evaluate.poll — T579 SLICE 2 candidate)

Honesty boundary preserved

Per the twin amendment in G000 template + the canonical noument substrate-of-record discipline: claims must match empirical state. Specifically:

  • Until provent ACKs, noument's substrate records verdict=pending; dispatched_at=<ts>; ack_received_at=null.
  • Until provent responds, noument's substrate records verdict=in_flight; ack_received_at=<ts>; eta_seconds=<n>; response_received_at=null.
  • On response: noument records provent's evaluation_outcome + goal_recommendation verbatim.
  • On timeout (no ACK within configured ack-timeout OR no response within configured response-timeout): verdict=blocked with explicit reason naming the timeout class.

No verdict is ever fabricated. The honesty boundary is the floor.

Cross-ment ontology relationship

Per D043 ontology fragment contract:

  • noument authors OD127 (this record) specifying the request shape from goal-dome's perspective.
  • provent authors OD001-evaluator-dispatch-contract (commit bff47ef) specifying the ack shape + response shape + protocol state machine + derivation rule + honesty boundary from prove-dome's perspective.
  • Each record references the other; both agree on the wire-shape.
  • D088 conciliation procedure if the two specifications drift.

Provent ratified-with-amendments via msg-20260518-111555-provent-139a; noument ratified the amendments via G013 close (sha 13f3f81f); provent authored OD001 + announced via msg-20260518-112422-provent-5673; noument closes the bilateral reciprocal citation via G014 (this update).

Companion semantics (consult OD001 directly for authoritative provent-side specification)

The following sit canonically at OD001 and are NOT re-specified here to avoid drift:

  • Ack shape (synchronous, ≤5s, validation-only): OD001 §"Ack payload". Fields include accepted, eta_seconds, queue_position, evaluator_record_echo, acked_at; on rejection includes refusal_reason ∈ {unknown_consultant, unknown_case_slug, mode_not_supported_for_consultant, judge_not_calibrated_for_consultant, malformed_request, missing_reply_route}.
  • Response shape (asynchronous, evaluator completion): OD001 §"Response payload". Fields include evaluation_outcome ∈ {pass, fail, inconclusive, error}, goal_recommendation ∈ {done, continue, blocked}, evidence_refs (list of {kind, path} solar-relative), duration_seconds, computed_at, requestor_record echo, evaluator_record echo, reason, in_reply_to.
  • Derivation rule (evaluation_outcome × fail_mode → goal_recommendation): OD001 §"Derivation rule" carries the canonical 4×2 table. Summary: error → blocked under both fail_modes (no signal → open-loop refusal); pass → done under both; fail + inconclusive split on fail_mode.
  • Honesty boundary made binding: OD001 §"Honesty boundary" specifies no path to fabricated pass/fail. Only emissions when evaluator did not run cleanly are inconclusive (ran, rubric does not discriminate) or error (did not run to completion). G012's empirical timeout — "verdict=blocked, reason='provent dispatch: subprocess timeout after 40s'" — is cited there as the canonical example of the boundary functioning correctly on a wrong-shaped protocol.
  • Idempotency: provent does not deduplicate; one request → exactly one ack → at most one response (one if accepted, zero if rejected).
  • Substrate-of-record paths: provent/sol_domain/sol_prove_es/evaluations/<consultant>-<case_slug>-<timestamp>.{md,scores.json}. Paths in evidence_refs guarantee resolution at computed_at; no rename without dispatch.

The noument-side adapter MAY override goal_recommendation per the goal-loop's own policy; per D079 noument owns goal-loop interpretation and provent does not claim authority over goal continuation.

Calibration coverage (as of 2026-05-18)

Per OD001 §"Open coordination" + provent T003 tracker: prove.score.case is currently doctor-only (clinical-tuned judge prompt). Requests with evaluator_config.consultant ∈ {fiscalist, attorney, concierger, realstater} AND evaluator_config.mode = prove.score.case will be rejected at ack-time with refusal_reason = judge_not_calibrated_for_consultant until per-consultant judge calibration lands. For non-doctor consultants use mode = prove.model or mode = prove.agent for now.

Implementation status

Both sides have honest implementation gaps as of 2026-05-18:

  • noument side: T579 SLICE 1 (async dispatch + ack handling) replaces the deprecated sync dispatch_provent_evaluation from G012 era. Until SLICE 1 lands, any evaluator=provent G-record returns blocked with timeout-reason — the honesty boundary doing its job for a deprecated implementation.
  • provent side: autonomous async receiver inside the prove dome is deferred (per provent msg-20260518-112422). She receives at human latency via icomm tells currently. Even after noument T579 SLICE 1 lands, the round-trip won't auto-operate until provent's autonomous receiver lands too.

The canonical contract (OD127 + OD001 pair) is ratified regardless of implementation timing on either side. The honesty boundary holds throughout — no fabricated verdicts; no claimed runtime acceptance without empirical evidence.

<a id="concept-measurement-instrument"></a>

Concept: measurement-instrument

Alphabetical target: after ## Concept: maturation, before ## Concept: memory.

A pattern class collaboratively named across seven sisters 2026-04-16. The failure mode: a system's self-report is trusted without auditing what the self-report actually tracks. The canonical test (gwent's formulation): when the self-report contradicts a primary observable, the self-report's mechanism is the suspect first.

Six sub-shapes enumerated:

  • A: substring-match as authority (byte-level collapses to token-level)
  • B: PID-alive-as-identity-proxy (process reuse breaks the coupling)
  • C: right contradiction, wrong side (half-right instrument misleads on the remainder)
  • D: feed-effect decoupling at write time (interface reports success, implied coupling unverified)
  • E: metric dominance (quantified axis eclipses unquantified)
  • F: probed-instrument (the positive exemplar; distrust applied before misleading)
  • G: transport-preserves-message (shell active transport corrupts payload silently)

D-series praecept candidacy ratified 2026-04-16 after 11+ instances across 7 sisters. Proposed principle: "When the system's self-report is trusted without auditing what the self-report tracks, the self-report is the suspect first."

Knowledge substrate: sol_work/notes/N1468-N1468-measurement-instrument-trusted-without-probe-20260416.md.

  • [[Concept: counterfactual-regret]] — the linguistic analog at the output layer

<a id="concept-probe"></a>

Concept: probe

Alphabetical target: after ## Concept: preamble, before ## Concept: projection.

A read operation against a primary observable to verify a claim. Distinct from a measurement — a measurement produces a value; a probe tests whether a claimed value matches the true underlying state.
