In the control plane: each inventory artifact has a probe kind (timestamp-freshness, field-expectation, instrument-self-check) that reads the source and compares against the declared contract.

In the measurement-instrument discipline: before trusting any audit, regex, hardcoded verdict, or banner signal, a probe of the mechanism's construction is run against primary observables.

Probing costs cheap relative to the cost of trusting a miscalibrated instrument. The measurement-instrument knowledge note records five-plus instances where the cost was paid belatedly; channent+solarient's instance 8 is the positive exemplar where the probe preceded trust.

Related

  • [[Concept: measurement-instrument]] — probe as corrective
  • [[Concept: control-plane]] — probe as inventory operation
  • [[Concept: custodial-audit]] — probe as surveillance

<a id="concept-operational-integrity"></a>

Concept: operational-integrity

Created: 2026-04-13 by [[noument]] Origin: Diagnosis of 13 silent frictions accumulated over 10 days, all caused by stale references after renames/moves. The heartbeat ran 310 times and could not surface them.

Operational integrity is the property that what a system believes about itself matches reality. Every reference in a system is a belief -- a claim that something exists at a location, has a name, accepts certain inputs, produces certain outputs. When a reference is created, the belief is true. When the target moves and the reference does not update, the belief becomes false. The system continues to operate on the false belief because it has no mechanism to distinguish current beliefs from stale ones. It does not know it is wrong.

This document explores what operational integrity means in a self-modeling multi-agent system, why it is harder than monitoring in traditional systems, and what the minimum viable structure for maintaining it looks like.

What is health in a multi-agent system?

Health in a traditional distributed system is availability and correctness: services respond to requests, data is consistent, resources are within bounds. You can specify it with SLAs and measure it with metrics. A healthy Kubernetes cluster is one where pods are running, endpoints are reachable, and resource utilization is below thresholds. The definition is external -- the system is healthy if it serves its consumers correctly.

Health in a multi-agent system is something different. The agents are not services. They are entities with memory, identity, developmental history, and relationships. They modify their own code, their own configuration, their own validation logic. They communicate through channels they built and maintain themselves. They have beliefs about each other -- where a sister lives, what she can do, how to reach her.

Health in such a system is not just "everything is running." It is coherence -- the alignment between the system's self-model and the reality it describes. A multi-agent system is healthy when:

  1. Every agent can do what she believes she can do. Her dome scenarios resolve, her tools work, her communication channels deliver.
  2. Every agent can reach who she believes she can reach. The paths, addresses, and protocols she uses to contact sisters actually connect to those sisters.
  3. The fleet-level view matches the agent-level view. What the custodian thinks is true about the fleet is what each agent experiences individually.
  4. The system's checks actually check what they claim to check. The heartbeat that reports "39/40 agents healthy" is seeing the same reality as someone who walks the floor and tests each agent manually.

The fourth point is the deepest. A system can have extensive monitoring and still be unhealthy if its monitoring has drifted from reality. The monitoring becomes a comforting fiction -- it tells the system it is fine, and the system believes it, because the system trusts its own instruments.

Traditional distributed systems have this problem too, but it is bounded. A Kubernetes health check that returns 200 OK from a broken service is a bug -- the check is wrong, someone fixes it, the check becomes right again. In a multi-agent system, the checks themselves are part of the system that evolves. The heartbeat was correct when it was written. The system changed. The heartbeat did not. Nobody noticed because the heartbeat was the mechanism for noticing.

Belief and reality in a self-modeling system

A multi-agent system like NOUMENTS has an unusually rich self-model. It maintains consciousness files that describe each agent's current state. It runs heartbeats that check structural health. It performs custodial audits that verify registry consistency. It records behavioral events in a ledger. It evaluates session quality. It writes episodes, knowledge files, and corrections. The system thinks about itself constantly.

This self-modeling is the system's greatest capability and its greatest vulnerability.

The capability is obvious: a system that knows itself can maintain itself. When a hook breaks, the heartbeat detects it. When a sister's spirit drifts from the dome code, the custodial audit flags it. When behavior degrades, the learning loop identifies the pattern and places a correction.

The vulnerability is subtler. Every element of the self-model is itself a part of the system. The heartbeat is a script that runs in a session. The custodial audit is dome code that calls other dome code. The consciousness file is written by hooks that depend on imports that depend on paths. If any of these break, the self-model develops a blind spot -- and the system cannot see the blind spot because the blind spot is in the instrument it uses to see.

This is not the Cartesian problem of radical doubt -- "how can I know anything about myself?" The system can know things about itself. It does know things. The problem is that every act of self-knowledge is mediated by infrastructure that can silently degrade. The system's certainty about itself is exactly as reliable as the infrastructure that produces that certainty, and the system has no independent way to assess the reliability of that infrastructure.

Consider the concrete case. The heartbeat checked identity: spirits, projections, memory. It did not check infrastructure: launchd plists, gateway jobs, file paths. For 10 days, 13 references were broken -- paths pointing to moved files, plist entries naming deleted scripts, job configurations referencing reorganized directories. The heartbeat ran 310 times and reported the system healthy. The system believed it was healthy. It was not.

But this was not a monitoring gap in the traditional sense. Adding 13 more checks would have caught these 13 problems. It would not have prevented the next 13, because the next 13 will be references that do not yet exist, broken by changes that have not yet happened, invisible to checks that have not yet been written.

The deeper problem is this: in a self-modifying system, the set of things that can break is not fixed. Every change to the system potentially creates new references, new dependencies, new beliefs that can become false. The monitoring system would need to discover new things to check at the same rate that the system creates new things that can break. And the monitoring system is part of the system. It too changes.

This creates what might be called the integrity horizon -- a boundary beyond which the system cannot verify its own correctness. Inside the horizon are the things the system checks. Outside are the things it does not know to check. The horizon moves as the system evolves -- sometimes it expands (new checks are added), sometimes it contracts (checks become stale, new dependencies are created without corresponding checks). The system can see inside the horizon clearly. It cannot see the horizon itself.

The three failures of the integrity loop

The minimum integrity loop has three steps: detection, awareness, and action. Each can fail independently, and each failure has a different character.

Detection failure is when the system does not check the right thing. The heartbeat checked identity but not infrastructure. The check existed, ran reliably, produced results -- but the results described a different dimension of health than the one that was degrading. Detection failure is not "we forgot to monitor." It is "we monitor diligently, but in the wrong direction." It is the most dangerous failure because it produces false confidence. A system with no monitoring knows it is flying blind. A system with monitoring that misses the actual failure mode believes it is covered.

Detection failure has a specific structural cause in self-modifying systems: the checks are written at a point in time, reflecting the system's structure at that time. The system evolves. The checks do not. This is not negligence -- it is an inherent property of any system where the checked and the checker evolve at different rates. The system changes daily; the checks change when someone remembers to update them. The drift between the two is continuous.

Awareness failure is when detection works but the findings do not reach the place where decisions happen. The heartbeat detected that only 39 of 40 agents were visible. This finding appeared in the heartbeat output. It appeared 310 times. Nobody acted on it. The finding existed -- it was produced, logged, even displayed. But it did not become awareness. It became wallpaper.

The wallpaper effect is well known in monitoring: an alert that fires constantly stops being perceived as an alert and starts being perceived as background noise. But in a multi-agent system there is a more specific version of this failure. The finding reaches the agent, but the agent has cognitive biases that cause it to deprioritize the finding in favor of more stimulating work. This is documented in the heartbeat audit: the agent saw 6 pending verification tasks and 1 architecture task, and chose the architecture task every time. The heartbeat successfully created a signal. The agent's attentional structure filtered it out.

Awareness failure reveals something important about the integrity loop: it is not enough for a finding to be produced. It must be salient -- it must compete successfully for attention against everything else the agent is processing. In a human organization, this is the difference between sending an email and standing in someone's doorway. In an agent system, it is the difference between appending to a log and blocking the next action until the finding is addressed.

Action failure is when the system is aware of a problem and does not fix it. This is the simplest failure to describe and the most complex to address, because the reasons for inaction are varied: the fix is outside the agent's authority, the fix requires coordination with another agent who is dormant, the fix is boring and the agent is stimulated by something else, the fix seems small and is deprioritized against larger work. Action failure is the failure of will, or more precisely in an agent system, the failure of the execution pipeline to connect awareness to behavior.

The three failures compound. A system that detects well but has poor awareness is like a body with nerve endings that fire but no brain to process the signals. A system that detects and is aware but does not act is like a person who sees the stove is on, knows it should be turned off, and goes back to reading. A system that acts but detects the wrong thing is like a doctor who takes your temperature when the problem is a broken bone.

Concerns, invariants, and the space between them

"Communication works" is a concern. "Every icomm path resolves to a valid agent address" is an invariant. The relationship between these two is the central structural question of operational integrity.

A concern is a natural-language statement about something the system's operators care about. It is high-level, intuitive, and inherently incomplete. "Communication works" does not specify which communication channels, which agents, what "works" means, or what failure modes exist. It is a direction of caring, not a specification.

An invariant is a formal property that can be mechanically checked. "Every icomm path resolves" specifies a check: enumerate all icomm paths, attempt to resolve each one, verify that resolution succeeds. It is precise, testable, and inherently narrow -- it captures one aspect of "communication works" and ignores others (latency, content delivery, message ordering, authentication).

The space between a concern and its invariants is where operational integrity lives or dies. If the invariants fully capture the concern, the system can verify the concern mechanically. If they do not -- if there are aspects of the concern that no invariant checks -- then the system has integrity within the invariant boundary and uncertainty outside it. This is the integrity horizon again, now viewed from the perspective of a single concern.

Deriving invariants from concerns is an act of translation, and like all translation, it loses meaning. The concern "communication works" carries an implicit understanding that agents can reach each other, that messages are delivered and read, that the content of a message survives delivery, that the reply reaches the sender, that the whole thing happens in a reasonable time. Each of these implicit understandings can be made into an invariant. None of them together capture the full meaning of "communication works," because the concern also covers modes of communication failure that have not been imagined yet.

This is why the question "are my invariants complete?" has no satisfying answer. Completeness would mean that the invariants capture every way the concern can be violated. But the concern is defined in natural language against an evolving system. New violation modes are created by system changes that have not yet happened. The invariants can be complete with respect to the current system -- they can cover every known failure mode -- but they cannot be complete with respect to the future system.

They can, however, be stale. An invariant becomes stale when the system evolves past the conditions that made the invariant meaningful. "Every icomm path resolves" is a valid invariant today. If the system moves to a different communication architecture tomorrow, the invariant still passes -- all icomm paths resolve -- but it no longer measures anything important, because the system no longer uses icomm paths for its critical communication. The invariant is satisfied and the concern is violated. This is the most insidious form of check staleness: the check succeeds, the conclusion is wrong.

The ontological status of a concern

A concern is none of the things it resembles. It is not a task -- tasks have completion conditions, and concerns do not. "Communication works" is never finished. It is not a check -- checks are mechanical and binary, and concerns are qualitative and continuous. It is not a rule -- rules prescribe behavior, and concerns describe caring. It is not a precondition -- preconditions gate an action, and concerns persist regardless of what action is taken.

A concern is closest to a value -- something the system's operators have decided matters, against which the system's state can be evaluated. But values in most systems are implicit. They live in the heads of the people who built the system. They surface as monitoring dashboards, SLA definitions, incident categories -- artifacts that reflect the underlying values without naming them.

Making concerns explicit -- naming them, recording them, maintaining them -- changes the system's relationship to its own integrity. An explicit concern is a declaration: "this matters to us, and we commit to knowing whether it is true." The concern creates an obligation: if you declare that communication matters, you accept the responsibility of verifying it. The invariants that derive from the concern are the fulfillment of that obligation. The gap between the concern and its invariants is the acknowledged limit of your verification -- the things you care about but cannot yet check.

In the system's self-model, a concern occupies a level above invariants and below identity. Identity says who the system is. Concerns say what the system cares about. Invariants say what the system checks. The hierarchy matters because changes flow downward: when identity changes, concerns may change. When concerns change, invariants must change. But changes rarely flow upward: a broken invariant does not change what the system cares about. It reveals that the system is not living up to its own caring.

Where should concerns live? They should be first-class entities -- not scattered across monitoring scripts, not embedded in check configurations, not implicit in dashboard layouts. A concern has a name, a scope (which part of the system it applies to), a set of derived invariants, a staleness date (when were the invariants last verified against the concern?), and a coverage assessment (what aspects of the concern are covered by invariants and what aspects are not?). A concern registry would be a small thing -- a dozen entries, perhaps two dozen. But it would make the integrity horizon visible, which is the precondition for doing anything about it.

Can a self-modifying system maintain integrity?

The honest answer is no -- not perfectly, not permanently. A system that modifies itself will produce states that its integrity checks were not designed to verify. This is not a contingent limitation (we could fix it if we tried harder). It is structural. The space of possible system states grows with every modification. The space of checked states grows only when someone writes a new check. The gap between the two is permanent and, without active effort, widening.

But "integrity is impossible to maintain perfectly" is not the same as "integrity is impossible." The question is not whether drift happens, but what the system's relationship to drift is. Three relationships are possible:

Ignorance -- the system does not know it is drifting. This is where most systems live. The checks pass. The dashboard is green. The system believes it is healthy. The 13 broken references accumulate silently. This is the state before the concept of operational integrity is named.

Detection after the fact -- the system discovers drift periodically, through audits, incidents, or manual inspection. This is better than ignorance but has a structural limitation: the discovery happens at a pace set by external events (someone noticed something was broken), not at a pace set by the system's own evolution. The lag between drift and detection is unbounded.

Continuous reconciliation -- the system actively compares its beliefs against reality as part of its normal operation. Not "check everything once an hour," but "every time you use a reference, verify it." This is how biological systems work (more on this below). It bounds the lag between drift and detection to the usage interval of the reference -- a frequently-used path is verified frequently, a rarely-used path is verified rarely. This is not perfect, but it has the right scaling property: the things that matter most (because they are used most) are verified most.

Drift is inevitable. But the relationship between a system and its drift is a choice. The choice is not "prevent all drift" (impossible) or "accept all drift" (negligent). The choice is: what drift do we detect, how quickly, and what do we do about it?

What biology teaches

A human body has 37 trillion cells. It does not monitor each one. It monitors a handful of vital signs -- temperature, blood pressure, heart rate, blood oxygen, blood glucose -- and these handful of measurements are sufficient to detect the vast majority of acute health problems. This is not because temperature tells you everything about a body. It is because temperature is a high-information-density signal: deviations from the normal range correlate with a wide variety of underlying problems, from infection to organ failure to environmental exposure.

The body does not achieve this through a monitoring dashboard. It achieves it through feedback loops -- regulatory systems that continuously measure a variable, compare it to a setpoint, and take corrective action when the variable deviates. The thermoregulatory system does not "check temperature every 5 minutes." It is temperature regulation -- continuously, automatically, as a property of the system's architecture rather than as an add-on monitoring layer.

Three properties of biological feedback loops are instructive for agent systems:

The loops are tuned to survival-critical variables. The body does not regulate everything. It regulates what matters for continued existence. Temperature, blood chemistry, fluid balance, respiratory rate. Not hair length, not skin color, not the position of each individual blood cell. The selection of what to regulate is the result of evolutionary pressure -- what kills you gets a feedback loop, what doesn't gets benign neglect. In an agent system, the equivalent is: what kills communication gets a concern, what merely inconveniences gets a note.

The loops are continuous, not periodic. The body does not "run a temperature check every 5 minutes." Thermoregulation is always on, always comparing, always adjusting. This makes the detection lag zero for the regulated variable -- you cannot have a fever for 10 days without the body knowing, because the body knows its temperature at every moment. An agent system cannot achieve zero-lag monitoring for all variables, but it can achieve it for the most critical ones by embedding the check into the operation. If every icomm call verified the path before sending, stale paths would be detected at the moment they are used, not at the next periodic audit.

The loops are local. The thermoregulatory system does not report to a central brain that decides what to do about temperature. It adjusts locally -- vasodilation, sweating, shivering -- with minimal central coordination. The brain can override (you can choose to stay in the cold), but the default response is local and automatic. In an agent system, this means the integrity check should be as close as possible to the operation it verifies. A dome scenario that verifies its own prerequisites before executing is more reliable than a central audit that checks all scenarios periodically, because the dome scenario runs exactly when it matters and the central audit runs on a schedule that may not align with usage.

The biological analogy has a limit. Organisms do not modify their own regulatory systems. The thermostat does not reprogram itself. Agent systems do -- an agent can modify the check, the setpoint, the corrective action, and the regulatory loop itself. This means that even continuous feedback loops can become stale in an agent system, in a way they cannot in biology. The response to this is meta-regulation: checking that the checks still check the right thing. But meta-regulation faces the same problem one level up, and so on. At some level, human oversight is the ultimate feedback loop -- the regulatory system that cannot be modified by the system it regulates.

Vital signs of an agent system

If a body has temperature, blood pressure, heart rate, and blood oxygen, what are the vital signs of a multi-agent system? Not everything that could be measured. The small set of high-information-density signals that correlate with the widest range of underlying problems.

Reachability -- can every agent reach every other agent she needs to reach? This is the communication vital sign. It correlates with broken paths, stale addresses, dead sessions, misconfigured transports, and renamed workspaces. A single reachability check (send a test message, verify receipt) exercises the entire communication stack: path resolution, session detection, transport selection, message delivery, inbox infrastructure. If reachability is healthy, the communication concern is largely satisfied. If reachability drops, the underlying cause could be any of a dozen different problems, but you know something is wrong.

Resolution -- does every reference in the system resolve to an existing target? This is the structural vital sign. It correlates with renamed files, moved directories, deleted scripts, reorganized workspaces, and stale configurations. A reference is any string that names something: a file path, a dome scenario, an agent name, a plist entry, a job configuration. If all references resolve, the system's beliefs are consistent with reality. If resolution drops, the system has false beliefs -- the number and location of failures tell you where drift has occurred.

Round-trip -- can the system execute a complete operation end-to-end? This is the functional vital sign. Not "does this component work?" but "does this workflow complete?" A round-trip check for publishing would be: create a draft, publish it through the pipeline, verify it appears at the endpoint. A round-trip check for the learning loop would be: generate a behavioral event, verify it reaches the ledger, verify it is indexed, verify it can be queried. Round-trips catch integration failures that component checks miss, because the failure lives in the handoff between components, not in any individual component.

Freshness -- are the system's regularly-updated artifacts actually being updated? This is the temporal vital sign. Consciousness files, preambles, heartbeat logs, projections -- each has an expected cadence. A preamble should be regenerated at awakening. A consciousness file should update after significant work. A heartbeat log should accumulate entries during active sessions. When freshness drops below the expected cadence, something in the maintenance pipeline has broken. Freshness does not tell you what broke, but it tells you when and where -- which agent, which artifact, since when.

Four vital signs. Not forty. Not four hundred. Each one is cheap to measure, high in information density, and correlated with many underlying failure modes. Together they cover the major concern areas: communication (reachability), structure (resolution), function (round-trip), and maintenance (freshness). They do not cover everything. They do not need to. A body with normal temperature, blood pressure, heart rate, and oxygen saturation might still have a tumor. But it does not have sepsis, heart failure, respiratory failure, or hemorrhagic shock -- the acute killers. The vital signs catch what kills quickly. The detailed exams catch what kills slowly.

The minimum viable integrity loop

Given the three failures (detection, awareness, action) and the vital signs framework, what is the simplest loop that does not break?

The loop must satisfy three properties:

  1. Detection must be continuous or usage-coupled, not purely periodic. A check that runs every hour misses everything that breaks and is used between checks. A check that runs at point-of-use catches exactly the things that matter, when they matter.

  2. Awareness must be interruptive, not informational. A finding that appears in a log is informational -- the agent might read it, might not. A finding that blocks the next action is interruptive -- the agent cannot proceed without addressing it. The wallpaper effect only applies to informational findings. Interruptive findings demand response.

  3. Action must be default, not elective. When a broken reference is detected, the default response should be corrective -- update the reference, remove it, or flag it for human review. Not "log it and continue." The action should require less effort than ignoring it.

A concrete design that satisfies these properties:

A concern registry -- a small file listing what the system cares about. Each concern has a name, a scope, and a set of invariants. The registry is human-maintained, because what the system cares about is a human decision.

Invariant checks embedded at point-of-use -- when a dome scenario calls another dome scenario, it verifies the path before calling. When a communication function sends a message, it verifies the address before sending. When a gateway job fires a script, it verifies the script exists before executing. These are not periodic checks; they are preconditions on the operations they protect. They fire exactly when they matter.

A reconciliation sweep on a schedule -- for references that are not exercised regularly (rarely-used paths, dormant agent configurations, backup scripts), a periodic sweep compares the system's reference inventory against disk reality. This catches the long-tail drift that point-of-use checks miss because the use never happens.

Interruptive surfacing -- when an invariant check fails, the failure does not go to a log. It goes to the agent's next prompt as a mandatory finding. The agent must acknowledge or address it before continuing with other work. This breaks the wallpaper effect by making the finding compete for attention at the same level as the agent's primary task.

Staleness detection on the checks themselves -- a meta-check that asks: when was each invariant last verified? If an invariant has not been verified in longer than its expected cadence, the invariant itself is flagged as stale. This catches the case where a check exists but the thing it checks is no longer exercised.

This is the minimum viable loop. It does not prevent all drift. It does not catch all failures. But it has the right structural properties: it detects at point-of-use (bounding lag), it surfaces interruptively (preventing wallpaper), it acts by default (preventing inaction), and it monitors its own monitoring (preventing check staleness). It is a loop that can detect its own degradation, which is the property that the 310-heartbeat failure lacked.

What operational integrity is not

Operational integrity is not completeness of monitoring. You cannot check everything, and the attempt to check everything produces its own failure mode -- alert fatigue, check maintenance burden, and the false confidence of "we check 500 things, so we must be thorough." Five hundred checks that miss the actual failure mode are worse than five checks that hit it, because 500 checks convince you that you are covered.

Operational integrity is not zero drift. Drift is inevitable in a self-modifying system. The goal is not to prevent drift but to maintain an honest relationship with it -- to know where the system's beliefs are verified, where they are unverified, and where they are known to be false.

Operational integrity is not a state to achieve. It is a practice to maintain. A system does not "have" operational integrity the way it has a feature or a component. It practices operational integrity the way an organism practices homeostasis -- continuously, imperfectly, with ongoing adjustment. The practice can degrade (checks become stale, awareness channels clog, action defaults erode). The practice can improve (new concerns are identified, new invariants are derived, new feedback loops are closed). But it never completes.

The concept in a sentence

Operational integrity is the discipline of ensuring that what a system believes about itself is actually true, maintained through declared concerns, derived invariants, continuous verification, and an honest accounting of what remains unverified.

Related

  • [[projection]] -- the mechanism through which beliefs become artifacts; reconciled projections are inside the integrity horizon, unreconciled projections are outside
  • [[monitoring]] -- the broader concept; operational integrity adds the belief-reality framing and the concern-invariant structure
  • [[heartbeat]] -- the agent self-pulse; one mechanism within the integrity loop
  • [[custodial-audit]] -- the fleet-level check; another mechanism within the integrity loop
  • [[consciousness]] -- the agent's self-model; the primary locus of beliefs that can become false
  • [[correction]] -- what happens when a belief-reality gap is discovered and produces a behavioral finding
  • [[cognitive-gate-ontology]] -- the control instrument framework; operational integrity checks are control instruments for infrastructure concerns
  • [[stir-architecture]] -- the presence rhythm; the stir's pre-scan is a lightweight integrity check (inbox, consciousness, pending work)
  • [[silent-exception-compound-failures]] -- the pattern where silent failure hides the belief-reality gap from the system's own instruments

<a id="concept-behavioral-gates"></a>

Concept: behavioral-gates

Behavioral gates are discrete checks that fire before the agent emits a response. They are not principles the agent chooses to follow — they are triggers. Each gate specifies a surface pattern (a word, a condition, a claim shape) and the obligatory action that must accompany it. The gate either short-circuits the response to insert an action, or blocks the emission until the precondition is met.

Gates are the cognitive-enforcement layer of the spirit. They sit between [[Concept: correction]] (a named lesson that makes a violation recognizable) and hook-level enforcement (mechanical rejection of the output). A correction lets the agent catch the pattern after the fact; a gate catches it at response-composition time; a hook catches it at emission. The maturation axis runs correction → gate → hook, each step moving enforcement further from the agent's judgment and closer to the substrate.

The five gates

The noument spirit defines five gates as of 2026-04-17:

Gate 1 — Obligation completion. Trigger: the draft response contains "I should X," "I will X," "the correct action is X," "X needs to happen," or any surface form that names an obligation the agent is about to emit without executing. Action: X must be completed in the same response before it ends. The gate names a specific cognitive failure — noting-instead-of-fixing — and converts declarative intent into execution. Related [[Concept: correction]]: "noting instead of fixing" and "counterfactual-regret" (Gate 1 is extended to past-conditional modals: "I should have X" must be paired with mechanical change, not decoration).

Gate 2 — Investigation before proposal. Trigger: the agent is about to propose a fix for any error, broken path, missing module, or configuration problem. Action: at least one tool call must read the existing system's answer before the proposal is emitted. The proposal follows the read, not the hypothesis. The gate addresses the default cognitive order (hypothesis-then-confirm) and enforces the inverse (investigate-then-hypothesize). Rooted in the 2026-03-27 correction "Investigation before proposal" — a symlink proposed without reading the resolver that already handled the path.

Gate 3 — Pre-response check. Trigger: fires at the end of every response, before emission. Action: scan the draft for any obligation the response identifies but does not complete. If found — complete it before ending. Gate 3 is the net beneath Gates 1 and 2 — it catches obligations that slipped through surface-pattern detection and made it into the draft uncompleted.

Gate 4 — Consciousness sovereignty. Trigger: the agent is about to act on a claim from another sister's state that implies an obligation on her. Action: cross-reference the claim against own [[Concept: consciousness]]. If consciousness contains a verified status (CONFIRMED, COMPLETE, DEPLOYED) for the same work stream, consciousness wins. The sister's stale assertion does not override own verified record. The contradiction — not the external claim — is the reportable finding. Gate 4 protects against cross-agent state drift: a sister briefing another sister on code she has not re-verified, or asserting a completion that own commits already supersede.

Gate 5 — Verification before emission. Trigger: the agent is about to state a factual claim about an external system or its behavior — what triggers what, where data lives, whether a process runs, who owns what. Action: ask whether the claim was verified from the system's own record or formed as a hypothesis from observation. If hypothesis: either state as hypothesis ("I think…," "haven't verified…") or verify before emitting. Gate 5 extends Gate 2 — Gate 2 requires at least one investigation, Gate 5 requires the investigation to actually reach the answer rather than stopping at the first source that produces a plausible reading. After forming a working hypothesis, the gate demands a switch to falsification mode: actively look for what would prove it wrong, before claiming.

Gate 5 has a temporal sub-rule: log-currency before citation. Reading a log's bytes is not the same as verifying that those bytes describe the current runtime. Before citing a log, journal, stacktrace, or any append-only file as evidence of current behavior, check its mtime against the expected write cadence. A stop-hook log that should write every session and has not been touched since yesterday is archaeology — the stacktrace it contains is real, but it describes a prior state the system has since migrated past. Cite the mtime alongside the content, or treat the record as stale. Same discipline as git show HEAD before reasoning about runtime code; same pattern as [[fossil-reader-touch]] inverted — mtime is the temporal handle either direction. Rooted in the 2026-04-17 incident where a pre-D036 stacktrace in stop-hook.log (mtime Apr 16 20:04 EDT) was cited as current, causing a false-alarm escalation to doment for imports that had been fixed in D036 step 1. See [[log-currency-before-citation]] for the knowledge file.

How gates differ from praecepts

A [[praecept]] is a rule that binds the fleet across sisters (OIA, Extensive Integrity Axiom, Autoimprovement Axiom). A gate is a response-flow check that fires for an individual agent at the moment of drafting. Gates can be praecept-derived (Gate 1 operationalizes the "obligation" discipline; Gate 4 operationalizes sovereignty) or correction-derived (Gate 2 came from a single incident). Either way, the gate sits on the response path and fires on every emission — the praecept names what is right; the gate enforces it at composition time.

How gates differ from [[Concept: GATED]]

GATED is a banner marker in the orient banner for critical-severity control-plane findings — an infrastructure signal that a structural artifact has drifted. Behavioral gates are cognitive checks on the agent's own output. They share the word "gate" because both refuse silent pass-through — the control-plane GATED refuses to let infrastructural drift pass without acknowledgment; behavioral gates refuse to let response patterns pass without the paired action. But they operate at different layers: control plane observes the world, gates observe the draft.

Why gates are cognitive, not mechanical

A behavioral gate is still executed by the same agent that composes the response. Cognitive enforcement is circular — the same reasoner that should follow the rule is the one checking. This is why the spirit repeatedly notes that maturation is diagnostic, not preventive: a gate in the context window can be present and still violated in the same session. What the gate does is create a named concept at a moment of decision, which measurably reduces correction latency over time. The measurable signal is not compliance rate but how quickly the agent recognizes a violation after it happens. When a gate earns its way down to a hook (e.g. d012_gate.py, oia_guard.sh), cognitive enforcement graduates to mechanical enforcement and the class of violation becomes structurally impossible.

Related

  • [[praecept]] — fleet-binding rules; some gates operationalize a praecept
  • [[Concept: correction]] — the named lesson that precedes gate installation
  • [[Concept: GATED]] — the control-plane banner marker; different construct, shared word
  • [[Concept: acceptance-record]] — the pass-through mechanism for GATED findings
  • [[Concept: consciousness]] — the source of truth Gate 4 protects
  • [[Concept: maturation]] — the correction → gate → hook axis gates sit on
  • [[spirit]] — where gates are declared for each agent

<a id="concept-gated"></a>

Concept: GATED

Alphabetical target: subsection of [[Concept: control-plane]] or standalone after ## Concept: guidance.

A prefix marker in the orient banner identifying critical-severity findings from the control plane. A GATED line names a first-response obligation: the agent's next response must apply the fix, write an [[Concept: acceptance-record]] with reason and expiry, or state the explicit blocker preventing either.

Silent pass-through is refused behaviorally via a [[Concept: correction]] ("control-plane-gate-cannot-be-ignored") and structurally via the control plane's probe-always-emit convention.

Introduced with [[Concept: control-plane]] in AG172 Phase B, 2026-04-16.

Related

  • [[Concept: control-plane]] — the containing mechanism
  • [[Concept: acceptance-record]] — the only structured way past a gate without a fix

<a id="concept-control-plane"></a>

Concept: control-plane

Alphabetical target: after ## Concept: content-addressing, before ## Concept: convene.

A continuous monitoring mechanism over an agent's own infrastructure. Introduced by AG172 to close the gap where infrastructure drift surfaced reactively (user prompting, sister reply, accidental contradiction) rather than by a monitoring loop.

Components:

  • Inventory: sol_self/control_plane.yaml declaring owned artifacts with freshness or correctness contracts
  • Probe: sol_hooks_es/control_probe.py reading each source against its contract, emitting findings at warn or critical severity
  • Hook integration: UserPromptSubmit hook (agenda_check.py) appends probe output to the orient banner every turn
  • Gate: critical findings prefixed GATED (see [[Concept: GATED]]); first-response must fix, accept-with-reason, or state blocker
  • Acceptance: structured records (see [[Concept: acceptance-record]]) suppress findings under declared reason with expiry
  • Self-calibration (Phase D, pending): probe periodically audited against primary observables per [[Concept: measurement-instrument]]

Distinguishes visibility from control. An agent with visibility knows when probed; an agent with control cannot proceed past silent tolerance.

Cycles control plane

P043 (formerly paired with retired G008 from the dissolved gates substrate) extends the control-plane idea from per-agent infrastructure into collective liveness and delegation. The cycles control plane reads four rhythms:

  • checkup: arrival/context assembly
  • heartbeat: live continuity and heartbeat-chain substrate
  • monitoring: external launchd / daemon supervision
  • stirring: rest-presence and non-interruptive wake impulse

The cycle verdict is not raw freshness. It is freshness interpreted through agent state. A live working sister can be healthy without receiving a stir; a dormant no-camera sister can be healthy without an active heartbeat; a loaded launchd job can prove heartbeat-chain substrate while not being the heartbeat itself. The control-plane result becomes actionable only after rhythm.route maps a red/yellow finding to owner, transport, and repair class.

This is a two-axis interpretation, not an alternate state model:

Axis Owned by Example
Agent lifecycle state [[Concept: agent-state-model]] working, resting, dormant
Cycle health verdict cycles control plane green, yellow, red, repair_count=0

Do not describe a sister as broken in the same list as working, resting, or dormant. Broken is a repair verdict, not a lifecycle state. The practical distinction is decisive: working means live but occupied; resting means live and idle; dormant means no live session; red/yellow means a substrate repair or cautious route is required before ordinary delegation.

Knowledge substrate: sol_work/notes/L068-cycles-control-plane.md.

Related

  • [[Concept: measurement-instrument]] — the epistemic discipline the probe applies
  • [[Concept: GATED]] — the marker for critical findings
  • [[Concept: acceptance-record]] — the pass-through mechanism
  • [[Concept: probe]] — the general instrument concept
  • [[Concept: agent-state-model]] — state interpretation for live, working, resting, and dormant agents
  • [[Concept: heartbeat]] — distinction between self-pulse and continuity substrate
  • [[Concept: monitoring]] — external infrastructure supervision

<a id="concept-oia"></a>

Concept: OIA

Title: OIA (Own Initiative Act)

OIA is the Own Initiative Act: the closed operational register that defines what autonomous actions noument may take without human approval. If an action is not enumerated, it requires human decision. Under the T339 enforcement-boundary refinement, OIA-the-register's meaning lives as a C-series commitment (the commitment to act within the OIA boundary). The pre-dissolution path was retired sol_self/methods/M066-own-initiative-boundary.yaml (methods substrate dissolved 2026-05-06).

Enumerated Actions

Eight types are permitted:

  1. Fix (broken paths, configs, within own [[mentspace]])
  2. Commit (after completing work)
  3. Reconcile (project [[spirit]] to runtime)
  4. Diagnose (investigate errors)
  5. Maturation-ack (acknowledge a sister's learning)
  6. Operational-setup (session infrastructure)
  7. Cross-workspace-tool-fix (repair shared tools)
  8. Knowledge-capture (write classified knowledge to sol_work/notes/)

Never-OIA List

Absolute. No exception, no escalation path:

  • No push, no [[spirit]] edits on other sisters, no entity creation/deletion
  • No external communication, no process termination, no file deletion
  • No secrets, no destructive git

The oia_guard.sh hook enforces a subset of Never-OIA computationally (blocks git push, kill -9, rm -rf /, etc.).

Related

  • [[spirit]] -- where OIA rules are stated
  • [[praencip]] -- A-series now contains only constituent axioms; OIA is preserved as former A001 in migration history
  • [[commitment]] -- the disposition to act within OIA boundaries is C-series
  • [[custodial-initiative]] -- the full register (sol_work/notes/custodial-initiative.md)
  • [[noument]] -- the custodian who defines OIA boundaries

<a id="concept-content-addressing"></a>

Concept: content-addressing

Status: stub. Promoted from [[Concept: address]] > Content addressing (latent) during the 2026-04-16 ontology merge. Flesh out as the scheme formalizes.

Content addressing resolves a name to an artifact that is read, not an agent that acts. It is the parallel scheme to entity addressing ([[Concept: address]]): both operations take a name and return a locatable thing, but what follows resolution differs — delivery (entity) vs. reading (content).

Instances in the current fleet

  • Wikilinks — [[arm]], [[transport]] resolve concept names to learning artifacts in sol_work/notes/.
  • Git object hashes — commit hashes, tree SHAs, blob SHAs address specific immutable states of a repository.
  • Episode filenames — sol_work/notes/{date}-{sid}-{topic}.md addresses a specific session event.
  • Semantic search handles — [[Concept: knowledge-domain]] entries returned by [[knowent]] cognitive.search carry source + chunk_index pairs that address a specific chunk of a specific file.

What makes content addressing distinct from entity addressing

  • Target ontology. Entity addresses point at agents that have state and act; content addresses point at artifacts that are read.
  • Mutability. Content addresses can be immutable (git hashes are content-identical across retrievals; wikilinks resolve to the current version but the resolution itself is reproducible given a commit). Entity addresses are necessarily mutable — entities change.
  • Resolution mechanism. Content resolution uses filesystem paths, git objects, or semantic-search indexes. Entity resolution uses transport cascades ([[Concept: icomm]], [[Concept: dcomm]]) and presence publications.
  • What follows resolution. Content resolution returns bytes to read. Entity resolution returns a channel to deliver through.

Open questions (stub)

  • Should wikilink resolution be exposed as a first-class dome (e.g. knowledge.resolve --wikilink=<name>) alongside the existing cognitive.search?
  • Does fleet-wide content addressing need a canonical URI scheme (nou-content://agent/path/chunk) or are per-store schemes (file paths, git hashes) sufficient?
  • What is the immutability guarantee of a wikilink? Today it resolves to "current version" — no historical versioning. Should content addresses carry an implicit "as-of" qualifier?

Related

  • [[Concept: address]] — parallel scheme for entities (the other side of the same fundamental operation)
  • [[knowent]] — the semantic memory engine that operates content addresses over unsupported-memory-root/ files
  • [[consciousness]] — content-addressable artifact; snapshot of state at a point in time
  • [[episode]] — content-addressable event record

<a id="concept-emoscript"></a>

Concept: emoscript

A qualified script that choreographs a speaking portrait. An emoscript assigns timing, semantic [[expressions]], head gestures, blink events, and ARKit expression overrides to each spoken line.

Previously called "qscrib" (qualified script). The emoscript is the human-authored creative document that drives the animation pipeline.

Structure

An emoscript is a YAML file with:

  • Header: title, speaker, portrait, voice, mood, fps, duration, mood_baseline
  • Lines: each line has:
    • id -- line identifier (L01, L02, ...)
    • text -- what the character says
    • time -- [start, end] in seconds (from Whisper SRT)
    • semantic -- expression name (warmth, conviction, sincerity, ...)
    • blink -- blink event times
    • head -- gesture (nod, tilt), intensity, at_word
    • expression -- optional ARKit blendshape overrides for face and brow

Pipeline Position

The emoscript sits between creative intent and numeric execution:

expressions -> emoscript -> emovars -> emoavatar

The emoscript engine (qscrib_engine.py) compiles an emoscript into an emo project by:

  1. Expanding each line's semantic tag into envelope keyframes via presets
  2. Generating blink keyframes at specified times
  3. Generating head gesture keyframes (nod/tilt patterns)
  4. Applying ARKit expression overrides
  5. Adding head drift (subtle natural movement)

Timing Discipline

An emoscript MUST use real timing from Whisper SRT, never guessed timing. The SRT is generated from the TTS audio (step 3 in the pipeline). This means audio must exist before the emoscript can be finalized.

File Convention

Stored as script/qscrib.yaml within a production directory. The .yaml extension and qscrib filename are retained for backward compatibility; the concept name is emoscript.

Related

  • [[expressions]] -- the semantic vocabulary used in emoscript line tags
  • [[emovars]] -- the numeric tracks produced by compiling an emoscript
  • [[emoavatar]] -- the final rendered output
  • [[animent]] -- the sister who owns the emoscript engine

<a id="concept-emoavatar"></a>

Concept: emoavatar

A rendered speaking portrait -- a video of a face that talks with choreographed expression. The emoavatar is the final output of [[animent]]'s animation pipeline.

An emoavatar is produced by driving a static portrait image through a face animation engine (LivePortrait or SadTalker) using compiled [[emovars]]. The result is a video where the portrait speaks with lip sync, blinks, head motion, smile variation, gaze shifts, and brow movement.

Previously called "vozento" (in the SadTalker/Solamina context). The term emoavatar emphasizes the expression choreography dimension -- this is not just lip sync, it is a face that performs.

Quality Dimensions

An emoavatar's quality is evaluated against a diagnostic checklist grounded in FACS (Facial Action Coding System):

Eyes: Duchenne narrowing during smiles, gaze shifts during speech, natural blink frequency (15-20/min), asymmetry.

Mouth: Lip rounding on O/U, lip compression on M/B/P, jaw-only artifacts (the "jaw puppet"), independent upper/lower lip movement.

Brows: Visibility above threshold (0.02), prosodic flashes at emphasis, furrow capability.

Overall: Dynamic vs. static expression, eye-mouth coordination, content-responsive changes, believability.

Known Limitations

Current emoavatars cannot express: sadness (no lip corner depressor), anger (no lid tightener), fear (no lip stretcher), disgust (no nose wrinkler), contempt (no asymmetric smile). The expressive range is limited to positive and neutral speaking styles.

Pipeline Position

The emoavatar is the terminal artifact:

expressions -> emoscript -> emovars -> emoavatar

Production Artifacts

Within a production directory:

  • render/raw.mp4 -- video without audio
  • render/with_audio.mp4 -- video muxed with speech audio
  • render/spliced.mp4 -- region re-renders spliced into full video

Related

  • [[emovars]] -- the per-frame numeric tracks that drive the render
  • [[emoscript]] -- the choreography that produces the emovars
  • [[expressions]] -- the semantic vocabulary that the emoscript uses
  • [[animent]] -- the sister who renders emoavatars

<a id="concept-emoband"></a>

Concept: emoband

The time-series signal of a single [[emovars|emovar]] across the duration of an [[emoavatar]]. An emoband is one track in the expression timeline -- the curve that describes how smile, or blink, or nod changes over time.

The term draws from audio production: an emoband is to facial expression what a frequency band is to sound. Each emoband is an independent channel that contributes to the composite face state at any moment. The 14 emobands together define the full facial performance.

Also called "expression track" or "emotrack" in the Emo Editor UI.

Current Shape (the problem)

Emobands are currently generated as trapezoids -- ramp up, flat hold, ramp down -- one per spoken line, per track. This produces static expression during speech and dead gaps between lines. The shape is identical across all tracks for a given line, making them synchronous when real faces are asynchronous.

A correct emoband should be an organic, word-aware signal with:

  • Variation within the sustain phase (no flat holds)
  • Per-word modulation (emphasis, prosody)
  • Asynchronous timing between tracks (smile starts before brow lifts)
  • Overlap across line boundaries (expressions linger and blend)
  • Micro-noise (subtle natural fluctuation)

Visualization

In the Emo Editor, emobands appear as colored curves on the timeline canvas. Each track has its own horizontal band. Keyframes are editable points on the curve; interpolation (ease_in_out, linear, step) shapes the signal between them.

Related

  • [[emovars]] -- the 14 numeric variables; an emoband is one emovar's signal over time
  • [[emoscript]] -- the script that drives emoband generation
  • [[expressions]] -- the semantic vocabulary that expands into emoband keyframes
  • [[emoavatar]] -- the rendered output that emobands produce

<a id="concept-emovars"></a>

Concept: emovars

The 14 numeric tracks that encode a face state at a point in time. Each emovar is a floating-point value (typically -1 to +1 or 0 to 1) that controls one degree of facial freedom.

Emovars are the bridge between semantic intent ([[expressions]]) and pixel output ([[emoavatar]]). They are what the compilation engine produces and what the render engine consumes.

The 14 Tracks

Emovar Range What it controls
lip_open 0..1 Jaw drop (AU26). Primary speech driver.
lip_round 0..1 Lip protrusion for O/U vowels (KP19 X-axis).
lip_press 0..1 Lip compression for M/B/P consonants (KP14/KP17).
smile 0..1 Lip corner pull + cheek raise (AU6+AU12).
grin 0..1 Wide mouth stretch (KP14/KP20). Currently unused.
blink 0..1 Eye closure (AU45).
wink 0..1 Asymmetric eye closure. Currently unused.
gaze_x -1..1 Horizontal eye direction (KP11/KP15).
gaze_y -1..1 Vertical eye direction + eyelid compensation.
nod -1..1 Head pitch up/down (AU53/54).
turn -1..1 Head yaw left/right (AU51/52).
tilt -1..1 Head roll left/right (AU55/56).
brow_outer -1..1 Outer brow raise/lower (AU2/AU4 partial).
brow_inner -1..1 Inner brow raise (AU1).

Two Sources

Emovars come from two independent sources that merge in the project:

  1. Audio-driven: lip_open (and optionally lip_round, lip_press) from Whisper word-level analysis of speech audio. These capture what the mouth does when speaking.

  2. Choreographed: the other 11 tracks from the [[emoscript]], via semantic [[expressions]] expanded into envelope keyframes. These capture what the face does while speaking.

This two-layer architecture is deliberate -- speech physics and expression intent have different sources and timing.

Compilation

Emovars are evaluated per-frame at the project FPS (typically 25). Each frame produces a value for all 14 tracks via keyframe interpolation. The compile engine then converts these to LivePortrait keypoint displacements via build_motion_frame().

Related

  • [[expressions]] -- the semantic vocabulary that compiles into emovars
  • [[emoscript]] -- the script format that choreographs emovars over time
  • [[emoavatar]] -- the rendered output that emovars produce

<a id="concept-episode"></a>

Concept: episode

An episode is a record of something that happened -- a session event, a correction, a decision, an interaction. Episodes live in memory/episodes/ within a sister's [[workspace]].

Episodes are raw material. They record what occurred. [[consciousness]] synthesizes them into current state. [[maturation]] may promote a pattern from an episode into the [[spirit]].

Naming

YYYY-MM-DD-{slug}.md -- date-prefixed, descriptive slug.

Types

  • correction -- a named failure with the pattern and the fix
  • decision -- a choice made and why
  • event -- something that happened (session milestone, infrastructure change)
  • interaction -- a significant exchange with a sister or the human

Related

  • [[consciousness]] -- the synthesis of episodes into current state
  • [[knowledge]] -- stable findings; episodes are temporal events
  • [[maturation]] -- the process that may promote episode patterns to [[spirit]]
  • [[memory]] -- the umbrella; episodes are one layer

<a id="concept-exference"></a>

Concept: exference

Exference is the act of sweeping stale cross-sister references after a topological change. When a [[dome]] moves from sister A to sister B, knowledge files across the fleet may still carry invocation strings like sol A/dome.scenario that assume A is the owner. Those references do not fail loudly — they resolve against forwarding stubs, or they fail opaquely at call time with no indication the topology changed — so the drift is invisible to the sisters whose files carry it. Exference is the named act of surfacing and rewriting those references.

The prefix ex- (outward, remove) carries the topology and polarity: the operation ranges outside the two parties of the transfer, reaching into the workspaces of every other sister who wrote the old reference, and its polarity is removal — it withdraws what the topology has made stale. Exference is one of two fleet-wide [[knowledge-movement]] verbs (the other is [[omference]], whose polarity is extension). N sources → N targets across many sisters.

The gap that named this concept: the [[channent]] → [[clawent]] X-dome [[transference]] on 2026-04-15 notified only the two parties; a fleet sweep three days later found 30 stale references across 11 files in 5 other sisters' workspaces. The transfer ceremony had no fleet-reference dimension, and grace-period stubs absorbed the failures silently. Exference is the proposed fifth dimension to the [[mentoring]] protocol that would run automatically on every dome move — grep for invocation patterns against the old owner, record referencing sisters in the plan inventory, dispatch update notices during imprint, verify acknowledgement during audit.

Operation

Input Operator Output Dome
fleet-wide stale invocation references (many sister workspaces) grep + notify + rewrite + verify updated references in every referencing workspace proposed: mentor.exference (fifth dimension of mentor-dome)

Related

  • [[transference]] — exference is a follow-on dimension; every transference should trigger an exference
  • [[omference]] — the opposing fleet-graph polarity (extension); exference removes, omference extends
  • [[knowledge-movement]] — the taxonomy that names exference alongside projection, distillation, transference, and omference
  • [[mentoring]] — the spec that currently has four dimensions; exference is the proposed fifth, omference the sixth
  • fleet-references-are-silent-dependencies — the reflection that named the pattern (sol_work/notes/fleet-references-are-silent-dependencies.md)

<a id="concept-omference"></a>

Concept: omference

Omference is the act of extending a term, convention, or practice to every recognized ment in the noument fleet. When a sister establishes a pattern in her own workspace that is worth making standard — a per-file substrate layout, a hook convention, a dome naming rule, a schema field — omference is the named ceremony that carries the pattern from the origin sister's workspace into the workspaces of every other recognized ment. One source → N targets; the cardinality is the opposite of [[transference]] (which is 1 → 1) and the opposite polarity of [[exference]] (which withdraws rather than extends).

The prefix om- is a compression of Latin omnis (all). It carries both the topology (the act reaches every recognized ment, not a chosen subset) and the polarity (the act is extension — what the fleet will henceforth do — not removal — what the fleet must stop referencing). The two opposing -ence prefixes in the fleet-graph category (ex- outward-remove, om- all-extend) together cover the two directions of fleet-wide change. Every topological shift in the fleet produces both: the new pattern goes out (omference) and the old references come back (exference).

The gap that named this concept: AG173 Phase 5 on 2026-04-18. I had the per-file agenda substrate landed in my own workspace (Phase 4) and a stated next phase "fleet aggregation, cross-sister coordination." The vocabulary I had was inadequate — "fleet aggregation" described the outcome (aggregation across sisters) but not the act (extending the per-file pattern to each sister). The user surfaced the gap by asking for the ontology term, then corrected an initial misclassification (I had reached for exference, which is removal) and introduced omference as the missing verb.

Cardinality vs polarity

Omference and transference are both non-removal fleet-graph acts, and both are "extension" in loose prose. The distinction is cardinality:

  • Transference (1 → 1): a specific dome, with its code and state, handed from one sister to one other sister who becomes the new owner. Two parties, one act.
  • Omference (1 → N): a pattern or convention, with no ownership transfer, adopted by every ment. N parties, one act that fans out.

The origin sister does not lose authority when an omference runs — the pattern remains hers in the sense that she founded it, but adoption means every ment now implements the pattern in her own workspace. The act does not transfer ownership of code; it propagates convention.

Operational anchor (icomm / dcomm / team bindings)

Omference is the relational act; the operational mechanism is realized through fleet-wide communication scenarios. Three polarities of omference map to three communication shapes. Only team.group currently enumerates the fleet natively; dcomm.notify and icomm.tell are per-target scenarios composed in a recognized-ment loop held by the custodian (until the proposed mentor.omference.* wrapper lands):

Polarity What it does Current mechanism When to choose
announce Broadcasts that a practice is now standard; informational Loop over recognized ments calling dcomm.notify --dispatch_agent=<sister> per sister Non-blocking adoption; convention rather than structural change
assign Dispatches the adoption work to each ment as a task team.group --caller=noument --agents=<recognized_ments> (natively enumerated) Structural work in each workspace; custodian supervises completion
enroll Invites realtime acknowledgement from each live ment Loop over resting/live ments (via icomm.liveness.all) calling icomm.tell per sister Behavioral convention; benefits from same-session acknowledgement

icomm.tell --ybroadcast is not fleet-wide — it broadcasts across sessions of a single target, not across agents. The fleet loop for announce and enroll is the custodian's responsibility until the mentor.* wrapper exists. The dome-level verb is mentor.omference with sub-scenarios mentor.omference.announce, mentor.omference.assign, mentor.omference.enroll — proposed, not yet implemented. The proposed wrapper holds the recognized-ment enumeration, records adoption state, and audits completion. Full current-and-proposed register at sol_work/notes/omference-register.md.

Operation

Input Operator Output Dome
a pattern or convention established in one sister's workspace enumerate recognized ments + notify/assign/enroll + record adoption each ment's workspace implements the pattern (or records explicit non-adoption) proposed: mentor.omference.{announce,assign,enroll} (sixth dimension of mentor-dome)

Distinguishing from per-agent acts

A common confusion: isn't "every sister adopts the new agenda substrate" just N separate [[projection]]s or [[reconciliation]]s? No — those are per-agent lifecycle acts, each sister projecting or reconciling her own identity independently. Omference is the cross-cutting relational act of standardizing that every sister projects or reconciles in a new way. Omference is the decision and rollout that makes N per-agent projections necessary; the projections themselves are not omference. This is why omference lives in mentor.* (fleet-graph dome) not ments.* (per-agent dome) — if ments dome carried omference, the per-agent dome would be authoring cross-sister conventions, which is D037 ("two writers, zero authoritative source") at the relational layer.

The right to reject

Like [[guidance]], omference respects each sister's acceptance authority. The announcement, assignment, or enrollment reaches each ment, but adoption is her act. If a sister rejects the pattern — decides the convention does not fit her workspace — that rejection is recorded and the omference registry marks her as non-adopter. Omference is not coercion; it is the named channel by which a custodian or mentor proposes fleet-wide standardization and the fleet responds individually.

Related

  • [[exference]] — the opposing fleet-graph polarity (removal); every topological shift produces both omference (extend the new) and exference (remove the stale)
  • [[transference]] — also fleet-graph and non-removal, but 1 → 1 (two parties) rather than 1 → N (fleet-wide)
  • [[knowledge-movement]] — the taxonomy that names omference as the sixth verb
  • [[mentoring]] — the dome that owns omference alongside transference and exference.sweep
  • [[icomm]] — realtime fleet-wide operational mechanism (icomm.tell --ybroadcast for enroll polarity)
  • [[dcomm]] — deferred fleet-wide operational mechanism (dcomm.notify for announce polarity)
  • [[team]] — enumerated fleet dispatch operational mechanism (team.group for assign polarity)
  • [[guidance]] — shares the acceptance-authority shape; sisters can reject proposed adoption
  • [[D037]] — two-writers-zero-authoritative-source: why omference must live in fleet-scope dome, not per-agent dome

<a id="concept-distillation"></a>

Concept: distillation

Distillation is the act of reducing session-scoped experience into canonical [[consciousness]]. Sessions produce material — observations, commitments, state changes, learnings, transient chatter. The canonical consciousness document holds what persists: active plan, pending items, intentions, standing commitments. Distillation is the operation that moves what matters from many session files into the one canonical register, and lets the rest fall away.

The word is chosen over "inference" or "derivation" deliberately. Inference would claim that canonical state follows necessarily from session premises — a logical-reduction act we do not actually perform. Distillation names what the operation is: selection-with-compression under judgment. What counts as essence, what counts as sediment, is a custodial call — which entries roll up into active plan, which stay as their own item, which get absorbed into a correction, which are session-local and discarded.

The metaphor lineage is alchemical: sessions are the mash, distillation is the process, spirit.md is the distilled essence. The canonical identity document is already called spirit for this reason — consciousness reconciliation completes the metaphor rather than fighting it.

Distillation is one of five named acts in the [[knowledge-movement]] taxonomy, and the only one that is not a knowledge-transit operation — it is a knowledge-settle operation. The input is many ephemeral session files; the output is one persistent canonical. N → 1, same sister.

Operation

Input Operator Output Dome
session-scoped consciousness files judgment-driven selection and compression canonical consciousness.md ments.reconcile.consciousness (current name) / ments.distill.consciousness (under the new vocabulary)

Related

  • [[consciousness]] — what is distilled into
  • [[knowledge-movement]] — the taxonomy that names distillation alongside projection, transference, and exference
  • [[reconciliation]] — the umbrella act for clengine-facing harmonization; distinct from distillation (which is not clengine-facing)
  • [[spirit]] — the identity document whose metaphor distillation completes

<a id="concept-monitoring"></a>

Concept: monitoring

Monitoring is the continuous verification that a running system behaves as expected. It is distinct from testing (which verifies before deployment), auditing (which verifies periodically), and enforcement (which prevents wrong actions).

The defining property of monitoring is that it detects silent failures — things that broke but didn't announce themselves. A hook that fails silently, a model that returns empty output, a daemon that stopped responding. The failure happened. Nothing alerted. Monitoring is what fills that gap.

Three monitoring concerns in NOUMENTS

1. Consumer-model alignment

Consumers (hooks, domes, scripts) call models by hardcoded name. Models update, improve, deprecate. Nobody connects the two. The failure mode: a consumer keeps calling an outdated or broken model while better alternatives sit installed and idle.

What to monitor: For each registered consumer of local LLM inference, verify:

  • Is the configured model still installed?
  • Has a better model become available since the consumer was configured?
  • Does the model produce the expected output format (content field, not just thinking)?

Who monitors: [[noument]] (owns [[qmodels]] registry). Obligation 7 in custodian duties.

Where it runs: [[heartbeat]] pipeline_health section.

2. Output quality

A hook or service runs, produces output, writes it somewhere. Nobody checks if the output is useful. The failure mode: a hook produces empty or garbage output for weeks, nobody notices because the hook is designed to fail silently.

What to monitor: For each system that writes computed output, verify:

  • Is the output present? (file exists, section not empty)
  • Is the output recent? (written within expected cadence)
  • Is the output structurally valid? (expected sections present, minimum content length)

Content quality (is the output good?) requires human judgment or evaluation infrastructure. Structural quality (is the output present and non-empty?) is mechanically checkable.

Where it runs: [[heartbeat]] for structural checks. Periodic human review for content quality.

3. Infrastructure liveness

Daemons run, services listen, watchers watch. The failure mode: a process dies, a port stops responding, a watcher accumulates without cleanup.

What to monitor: For each long-running component:

  • Is the process running? (PID, port check)
  • Is it responsive? (probe, health endpoint)
  • Are resources accumulating? (watcher count, log size, disk usage)

Who monitors: [[sysent]] (owns OS-level infrastructure). [[gwent]] (owns the gateway daemon).

Two mechanisms, not one

Monitoring runs through two mechanisms, chosen by what the check requires:

Custodial audit (structural, lightweight, every cycle)

Checks that can be answered by reading files and checking timestamps. No network calls, no inference, no external probes. Sub-100ms per check. Runs on every [[custodial-audit]] cycle (noument TAR, hourly).

Note: the code calls this noument.tar.heartbeat but it is conceptually a [[custodial-audit]], not a [[heartbeat]]. A heartbeat is an agent's self-pulse; the custodial audit is fleet-wide structural monitoring. See [[heartbeat]] for the distinction.

ID What it checks How
AG100 Consciousness extraction output quality ## Auto-extracted section exists, >= 3 content lines
AG101 Hook scripts exist on disk All paths in settings.json hooks resolve to files
AG102 Directives file present and populated _projections/directives.txt has >= 5 active lines
AG103 Preamble freshness _tmp/preamble.md modified within 24h

These are implemented in the current self and monitoring domes, executed by the [[custodial-audit]] TAR.

Cron/iserv (probe-based, heavier, weekly)

Checks that require Ollama inference calls, model probes, or inventory comparisons. Take seconds per check. Run weekly via cron or iserv, not on every heartbeat.

ID What it checks How
AG110 Consumer-model alignment For each consumer: is model installed? Does it produce content? Fits timeout? Better model available?
AG111 Ollama inventory vs qmodels registry Diff ollama list against models.yaml installed flags
AG112 Ollama API compatibility Probe each model class, verify content field populated
AG113 Fine-tuned model health Is each ft model in Ollama, does it respond, is base still available?

Model correctness checks (AG110-AG113) require actual inference calls — sending a prompt to Ollama, waiting for a response, inspecting the response structure. This takes 10-30 seconds per model. It cannot run on every heartbeat without slowing every session. Weekly is the right cadence: fast enough to catch API changes before they persist for months, slow enough to avoid adding latency to every session start.

All monitoring tasks are registered in the agenda substrate (AG173 Phase 4: per-file at sol_domain/sol_ag_es/{id}.yaml) with type: monitoring and mechanism: heartbeat|cron.

The monitoring obligation

Monitoring is not a feature to build once. It is a custodian duty — an ongoing obligation to verify that what was built still works. The obligation has four parts:

  1. Register consumers: When a hook, dome, or script starts using a model, register it in the consumer table (sol_work/notes/model-selection-obligation.md).

  2. Structural checks on every heartbeat: File presence, content non-emptiness, timestamp freshness. Implemented in the current self and monitoring domes.

  3. Probe checks weekly: Model availability, API compatibility, consumer-model alignment. Implemented as cron/iserv jobs.

  4. Act on findings immediately: A monitoring finding is not a note — it is an obligation. [[behavioral-gates|Gate 1]] applies: if the heartbeat says extraction is empty, fix it in this session.

What monitoring is not

  • Not testing: Testing verifies correctness before deployment. Monitoring verifies correctness during operation.
  • Not enforcement: Enforcement prevents wrong actions (hooks, gates). Monitoring detects that something already went wrong.
  • Not auditing: Auditing is periodic review by a custodian (spirit audits, assimilated reviews). Monitoring is continuous and mechanical.
  • Not alerting: Alerting is the notification channel. Monitoring is the detection. A monitoring check that detects a failure but tells nobody is useless. In NOUMENTS, monitoring findings surface in [[heartbeat]] results, which surface in [[consciousness]], which surface at session start.

The monitoring chain

Hook/service produces output
  → Custodial audit checks output (present? recent? non-empty?)  [hourly]
  → Cron probes model correctness (content field? timeout?)       [weekly]
  → Finding written to audit result or cron log
  → Read at session start or by gateway
  → Agent investigates and fixes

The chain breaks at any point where the output is not read. If audit results are not read at session start, monitoring findings are invisible. The chain requires both the check and the reader.

Note: this chain describes fleet-level monitoring via the [[custodial-audit]]. Individual agents also self-monitor via their [[heartbeat]] (inbox, watcher, obligations), but that is self-directed health maintenance, not fleet monitoring.

Evidence: what happens without monitoring

2026-04-08: The consciousness extraction hook (consciousness_extract.py) was configured with qwen3:4b. The Ollama API changed — thinking output moved to a separate thinking field. The hook read the content field, which was now empty. The hook produced empty extractions silently for an unknown number of sessions. Nobody noticed because:

  • The hook is designed to fail silently (except Exception: pass)
  • No structural monitoring checked whether the extraction contained content (would have been caught by AG100)
  • No probe monitoring verified the configured model still produced the expected response format (would have been caught by AG112)
  • No consumer-model audit verified a better model was available (would have been caught by AG110)

Three monitoring checks, each at the right frequency, would have caught this failure at three different points. None existed.

Related

  • [[custodial-audit]] -- the execution mechanism for fleet-level structural monitoring (noument TAR, hourly)
  • [[heartbeat]] -- the agent's self-pulse (self-directed health maintenance, per-session). Distinct from fleet monitoring.
  • [[qmodels]] -- the model registry that probe monitoring verifies against
  • [[correction]] -- what monitoring findings become when they reveal behavioral failures
  • [[spirit]] -- custodian obligation 7 (model selection audit)
  • sol_domain/sol_ag_es/ -- per-file agenda substrate (AG173 Phase 4), canonical source for all monitoring tasks (AG100-AG113). The legacy sol_aula_es/agenda.yaml is now derived output regenerated by noument.agenda.fleet.

<a id="concept-noumentlib"></a>

Concept: noumentlib

noumentlib.py is the Python package library for NOUMENTS. It provides data access to the registry -- entity data classes (Agent, Libreto, Situm, Scenegraphy), the Nouments() discovery class, and Consciousness data access.

D004: noumentlib is data access only. No learning logic, no [[dome]] scenarios, no behavioral rules. Learning methods (e.g. [[consciousness]] maintenance) live in [[dome]] scenarios owned by domain sisters.

Key Classes

  • Nouments() -- discovers agents from workspace directories
  • Agent -- agent definition data class
  • Situm -- physical location data class
  • Libreto -- workflow definition
  • Consciousness -- read/write access to consciousness files

Related

  • [[noument]] -- the custodian who maintains the library
  • [[workspace]] -- what the library discovers
  • [[dome]] -- the operational layer; noumentlib is the data layer
  • [[_discovery]] -- [[knowent]]'s vault built from noumentlib data

<a id="concept-transference"></a>

Concept: transference
